Notebook practice · Does the gradient see where the instruments were actually placed?

Chapter 11 · Constraints, sparse signals and edge-preserving penalties

Derive a solution at a bound, a soft-threshold rule and the behaviour of a total-variation penalty at a material interface.

From first principles

Derive thresholding from an absolute-value penalty

A coefficient that can physically vanish can be estimated by balancing squared measurement mismatch against a penalty on its absolute magnitude. For a scalar reading \(y\), consider \(F(x)=\tfrac12(x-y)^2+\lambda|x|\) with \(\lambda\ge0\). On either open half-line the derivative is ordinary, giving candidates

\[x=y-\lambda\quad(x>0),\]
\[x=y+\lambda\quad(x<0).\]

When neither candidate has the assumed sign, the kink at zero must be checked. The left derivative at zero is \(-y-\lambda\) and the right derivative is \(-y+\lambda\). A convex minimum occurs there if these enclose zero, namely if \(|y|\le\lambda\). Combining the cases gives

\[x_*=\operatorname{sign}(y)\max(|y|-\lambda,0).\]

The shrinkage creates exact zeros and biases surviving amplitudes toward zero. Applying a similar penalty to differences between adjacent field values instead of to the field itself favours neighbouring sites that share one value while permitting isolated jumps.

Derivation and applications

Shrink a two-dimensional gradient while preserving its direction

At an image pixel or a mesh cell the inferred gradient can have two measured components. If the physical surface has no preferred coordinate axes, a rotationally invariant local penalty is proportional to the Euclidean length of the gradient vector. Consider the local optimization with equally scaled and equally precise components,

\[\min_{g\in\mathbb R^2}\ \frac12\|g-y\|_2^2+\lambda\|g\|_2,\qquad \lambda\ge0 .\]

For a fixed trial length \(r=\|g\|\), the data mismatch is minimized by making \(g\) parallel to \(y\), since this maximizes their dot product. For \(y\ne0\) write \(g=r y/\|y\|\). The remaining one-dimensional constrained problem is

\[\min_{r\ge0}\ \frac12(r-\|y\|)^2+\lambda r,\qquad r_*=\max(\|y\|-\lambda,0).\]

Thus both gradient components are attenuated by a common factor; if \(y=0\), the solution is zero directly. The componentwise absolute-value penalty gives a different rule,

\[g_*=\max(1-\lambda/\|y\|,0)y,\qquad (g_{\rm component})_i=\operatorname{sign}(y_i)\max(|y_i|-\lambda,0).\]

The first expression is for \(y\ne0\). A local gradient shrinkage step is used inside some total-variation algorithms, but arbitrary independently shrunk gradient vectors need not be differences of one global image. A full reconstruction must enforce consistency with the field and the measurement equations. Different component units or uncertainties likewise require a corresponding metric.

Worked example

Compare an inclined edge under two penalties

For measured gradient \(y=(3,4)\) and \(\lambda=2\), the isotropic solution has length \(5-2=3\) and vector \((1.8,2.4)\); its angle is unchanged. Applying componentwise shrinkage gives \((1,2)\) with angle \(63.4^\circ\), compared with the measured \(53.1^\circ\). The choice of penalty can therefore affect an estimated orientation.

Second application

Test whether a detection threshold depends on the grid axes

A second gradient has length \(1.2\) with \(\lambda=1\). Along an axis, both rules return length \(0.2\). Rotating it by \(45^\circ\) gives coordinates \((0.849,0.849)\). The isotropic rule still returns length \(0.2\), whereas componentwise thresholding returns zero because neither coordinate reaches one. An orientation-dependent disappearance can therefore be introduced by the estimator itself.

Physical constraints change the optimality test

An attenuation coefficient cannot be negative. In a convex inversion with lower bound zero, the unconstrained least-squares normal equations need not hold at a valid constrained solution. Consider minimising a differentiable \(F(x)\) with \(x_j\ge0\). At a free component the derivative must be zero. At a zero component only positive feasible perturbations exist, so the derivative must be nonnegative. Negative reconstructed cells cannot generally be repaired by clipping after inversion because the forward operator couples neighbouring parameters.

For example minimise \(\tfrac12[(x_1+x_2-1)^2+(x_1+1)^2]\) under \(x_i\ge0\). The unconstrained solution is \((-1,2)\). Clipping gives \((0,2)\), which overpredicts the first observation. On the active face \(x_1=0\), the minimising \(x_2\) is 1. At \((0,1)\) the gradient is \((1,0)\) and the bound conditions hold. The price of feasibility appears in the data residual; it is important to show which observations the constrained model cannot fit.

Derive the soft-threshold rule

For a scalar coefficient with observation \(b\), minimise \(\tfrac12(x-b)^2+\lambda|x|\). On \(x>0\) the stationary point is \(b-\lambda\), feasible there only when \(b>\lambda\); on \(x<0\) it is \(b+\lambda\). At zero, the admissible slopes of the absolute value range from \(-1\) to 1, so zero solves the condition when \(|b|\le\lambda\). Together these give

\[x=\operatorname{sign}(b)\max(|b|-\lambda,0).\]

Even retained coefficients shrink by \(\lambda\), a real amplitude bias. With \(b=(-0.2,0.7,2.4)\) and \(\lambda=0.5\), the estimate is \((0,0.2,1.9)\) for an identity forward map.

Penalise changes in a field

In one dimension discrete total variation is \(\sum_j|x_{j+1}-x_j|\). A single large jump and several monotone small steps spanning the same overall change have equal total variation. In contrast a squared difference penalty favours spreading the jump over several sites. That explains why total variation can preserve an abrupt edge when the data localise it. It can also create nearly constant plateaus in a field that really changes smoothly.

For two observed values \(b_1,b_2\), use the variables \(m=(x_1+x_2)/2,\ d=(x_2-x_1)/2\). With objective \(\tfrac12\sum(x_i-b_i)^2+\lambda|x_2-x_1|\), the optimal mean is the observed mean and half-difference is the soft-thresholded observed half-difference with threshold \(\lambda\). When \(|b_2-b_1|\le2\lambda\) the two estimated levels fuse. This concrete loss of resolution should be reported when interpreting a uniform patch in a reconstructed image.

Make an edge claim testable

Generate test fields both with a sharp jump and with a smooth ramp. Pass them through the same sensor model and noise level, then fit with the same strength-selection procedure used for real data. Measure contrast bias and edge-location error separately; an image can put an edge in the correct place but suppress its amplitude. For a nonnegative material field inspect the gradient at active zeros alongside the residual. The regulariser and the bounds each supply information that the instrument may not have measured.

Follow total variation through three coupled cells

Consider three ordered cell values observed directly with equal independent precision. Minimise \(\tfrac12\sum_i(x_i-b_i)^2+\lambda(|x_2-x_1|+|x_3-x_2|)\). Introduce edge variables \(s_1,s_2\) equal to the signs of nonzero differences. At a zero difference either variable may take any value between minus one and one. Differentiation with these admissible subgradients gives the three balances \(x_1-b_1-\lambda s_1=0,\ x_2-b_2+\lambda(s_1-s_2)=0,\ x_3-b_3+\lambda s_2=0\). A candidate solution must satisfy all three balances and must agree with the sign choices that produced it.

Adding the balances eliminates both internal edge forces. Consequently the reconstructed values have exactly the same sum as the observations, regardless of the penalty strength. The quadratic part is strictly convex, so any feasible sign assignment satisfying the balances proves that the state is the unique minimiser. Adding lower bounds can introduce reactions and break the sum identity. Unequal precision also changes which weighted average is conserved. The calculation depends on the observation operator being the identity; after blurring, the first derivative of the data term already couples all cells.

Derive the complete fusion path for a short profile

Take \(b=(0,1,5)^T\). For small positive \(\lambda\), both differences remain positive, so

\[s_1=s_2=1.\]

The equations give

\[x=(\lambda,1,5-\lambda)^T.\]

The first two coordinates meet at \(\lambda=1\), while the second and third are still unequal. Assume those first two now form a plateau of height \(a\), with \(s_2=1\). Adding their balances gives \(2a-1-\lambda=0\), hence \(a=(1+\lambda)/2\). The first equation then requires

\[s_1=a/\lambda=(1+\lambda)/(2\lambda),\]

which lies in the permitted interval exactly when \(\lambda\ge1\).

The third coordinate stays \(5-\lambda\). Equating it to the plateau gives \((1+\lambda)/2=5-\lambda\), or \(\lambda=3\). Beyond this value all three cells coincide, and sum conservation fixes them at two. The required subgradients are \(s_1=2/\lambda\) and \(s_2=3/\lambda\), both admissible for \(\lambda\ge3\). This completes the path without numerical optimisation. At strengths 0.5, 2 and 4 the respective profiles are \((0.5,1,4.5)\), \((1.5,1.5,3)\) and \((2,2,2)\).

The thresholds record when the data imbalance inside a proposed plateau can be supported by the admissible edge subgradients. A flat patch at the second strength is therefore compatible with unequal raw values of zero and one. Conversely, a visible third-cell edge at that strength has already lost much of its amplitude. A reconstruction report that uses one threshold for declaring the existence of a jump and another for estimating its amplitude should explain both decisions.

Checking the final all-fused solution using only its observed mean would miss the second edge constraint. For example at strength two that constant candidate would require \(s_2=1.5\), outside the admissible unit interval, despite reproducing total mass perfectly. The failed local balance tells which boundary of the candidate plateau has to reopen. This provides a simple hand diagnostic for more complicated one-dimensional reconstructions when an apparently reasonable flat interval has been proposed.

Choose how weak coefficients enter a reconstruction

For unit forward gain, minimise half squared data error plus either \(0.5|x|\) or \(0.5x^2/2\). Solve the derivative equation away from zero and use the subgradient at zero for the absolute-value penalty. Here the plotted input is the measured datum.

Recovering sparse coefficients without hiding amplitude bias · Inferring parameters and fields from observations

A sensor records a linear mixture of a few active components. Derive the soft-threshold map, use it in an iterative reconstruction and decide when correlated response columns make an apparently sharp sparse answer ambiguous.

Chapter 12 · A reconstruction audit from observations to a defensible claim

Combine residuals, resolution, uncertainty and independent predictions to decide what one spatial experiment establishes.

From first principles

Audit a recovered contrast in measurement units

Suppose two equally calibrated channels sense neighbouring state values with symmetric cross-talk \(0\le a<1/2\). Their noiseless observations are \(y_1=(1-a)x_1+ax_2\) and \(y_2=ax_1+(1-a)x_2\). Subtracting removes the common level and reveals how strongly the desired contrast \(d=x_1-x_2\) appears in the readings,

\[y_1-y_2=(1-2a)d .\]

Independent zero-mean channel noise of common standard deviation \(s\) gives difference variance \(2s^2\). Inverting only this observable therefore yields the data-based estimate and uncertainty

\[\widehat d=\frac{y_1-y_2}{1-2a},\]
\[\operatorname{sd}(\widehat d)=\frac{\sqrt2\,s}{|1-2a|}.\]

The noise is amplified as the channels approach identical response. A useful resolution audit reports the measured difference, the calibrated contrast gain and the relevant uncertainty sources. If the gain itself is uncertain or channel noise is correlated, those effects must enter the audit before a physical contrast is claimed.

Background · Resolution matrix
\[R=G^\dagger_{\rm reg}G\]

For a linear regularised inverse, a row describes how the estimated parameter mixes the actual components. A sharp-looking result may still have broad resolution kernels.

Derivation and applications

Quantify signal leakage into an estimate of a local field

A reconstructed pixel is often a weighted average of several true cells. A resolution audit can predict that mixing and bound its effect using independent knowledge of admissible field gradients. Consider a linear model \(y=Ax+\epsilon\) with zero-mean measurement error of covariance \(\Sigma\), and a chosen linear estimator for the target cell \(j\), \(\widehat x_j=w^Ty\). Define its resolution weights by the response to unit signals in individual true cells,

\[k^T=w^TA,\]
\[\operatorname{E}\widehat x_j=\sum_i k_i x_i,\]
\[\operatorname{var}\widehat x_j=w^T\Sigma w .\]

To reproduce a uniform field exactly, require \(\sum_i k_i=1\). The bias relative to the target is then a sum of differences from that cell. Suppose independently supported spatial smoothness gives

\[|x_i-x_j|\le Ld_{ij},\]

where \(d_{ij}\) is cell distance. Taking absolute values yields

\[|\operatorname{E}\widehat x_j-x_j|=\left|\sum_i k_i(x_i-x_j)\right|\le L\sum_i |k_i|d_{ij}.\]

This weighted distance has units of length even when resolution weights have negative lobes. If Gaussian measurement noise is justified, a conservative two-sided interval with at least the usual 95 percent coverage for the true cell is

\[\widehat x_j\ \pm\left[L\sum_i|k_i|d_{ij}+1.96\sqrt{w^T\Sigma w}\right].\]

The bias bound is added to the statistical margin because it represents an unknown fixed error, not an independent random draw. The guarantee depends on the supplied smoothness information and calibrated forward model. At a genuine sharp interface the assumed gradient bound may be inappropriate.

Worked example

Audit a local mean on a uniform grid

Three true cell centres lie \(0.5\,\mathrm m\) apart. An estimator of the middle temperature has weights \((0.2,0.6,0.2)\), output noise deviation \(0.10\,\mathrm K\) and independently verified gradient bound \(2.0\,\mathrm{K/m}\). Its effective weighted distance is \(0.20\,\mathrm m\) and maximal mixing bias is \(0.40\,\mathrm K\). An estimate of \(18.0\,^\circ\mathrm C\) has interval half-width \(0.40+1.96(0.10)=0.596\,\mathrm K\).

Second application

Compare a sharper reconstruction at a noisy boundary

An alternative three-cell estimator has weights \((-0.1,1.2,-0.1)\) at distances \(1,0,1\,\mathrm m\). For \(L=0.50\,\mathrm{K/m}\) its bias bound is \(0.10\,\mathrm K\). With noise deviation \(0.30\,\mathrm K\), the total half-width is \(0.688\,\mathrm K\). A smoother estimator of the same target with weighted distance \(0.50\,\mathrm m\) and noise deviation \(0.12\,\mathrm K\) gives

\[0.25+0.235=0.485\,\mathrm K.\]

The sharper-looking estimator yields the wider interval for this stated field class.

Set a question that can fail

Two adjacent thermal zones are blurred by the sensor matrix \(A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0.55&0.45\\0.45&0.55\end{smallmatrix}\right)\). Let the observations be \(y=(21.08,20.92)\) °C with independent standard deviations 0.10 K. The engineering question is whether the first zone is at least one kelvin hotter than the second. Work in mean and difference coordinates. The mean passes unchanged, while the difference is attenuated by 0.10. Before fitting, predict that uncertainty in the contrast will dominate this decision.

Derive the data-only estimate and interval

The observed mean is 21 °C, with standard deviation \(0.10/\sqrt2=0.0707\) K. The estimated true contrast is

\[\hat d=(21.08-20.92)/0.10=1.60\,\mathrm{K}.\]

Its standard deviation is

\[\sqrt2(0.10)/0.10=1.414\,\mathrm{K}.\]

For known Gaussian noise, a two-sided 95% data-only interval is about \([-1.17,4.37]\) K. The inverse values \((21.8,20.2)\) exactly reproduce both readings. Nevertheless, even the sign of the contrast is uncertain at this level. The exact residual consumed both degrees of freedom and cannot test the instrument model.

Compare a prior-based inference on the same scale

Assume an independent prior for the contrast \(d\sim N(0,1\ \mathrm{K}^2)\), leaving the mean very weakly constrained. In contrast coordinates the data variance is 2 and the observed estimate is 1.60. Adding precisions gives posterior variance \(1/(1+1/2)=2/3\) K² and mean \((2/3)(1.60/2)=0.533\) K. The probability \(P(d>1\mid y)\) is \(1-\Phi[(1-0.533)/\sqrt{2/3}]\simeq0.28\). The prior changed both the centre and the width; this probability is explicitly conditional on the zero-centred prior. Repeating with a wider prior shows how much the conclusion depends on that assumption.

Buy information in the direction the decision needs

Suppose one additional test is available. Another independent repeat with the same blurred sensor reduces the data-only standard deviation of the contrast by \(\sqrt2\), to 1.0 K. A new differential sensor reading \(d+\epsilon\) with standard deviation 0.50 K and errors independent of the original instrument directly adds precision 4 K⁻² to the existing 0.5 K⁻², giving combined standard deviation \(1/\sqrt{4.5}=0.471\) K. This pre-data comparison depends on geometry and precision alone, not on an unknown future reading. Use held-back calibration objects to verify that the new sensor's stated precision and averaging model hold near an actual interface.

Communicate the final claim

Retain the observable mean and the uncertain contrast as separate reported quantities. State the noise law and model assumptions behind any interval. Where a numerical solver contributed, distinguish verified numerical precision from the statistical precision of the data. The difference is also visible in the temperature-design case: making the optimisation converge more tightly cannot restore information lost when all temperatures are close to the reference. A successful analysis may conclude that a particular observation needs redesign while still supplying an accurate prediction of another quantity.

Propagate a spatially varying calibration error

The preceding contrast calculation assumes a known blur and unbiased independent channel errors. Add two possible calibration terms: an offset \(b\) shared by both readings and a differential offset \(c\) that enters the first reading as \(+c/2\) and the second as \(-c/2\). The measurement equations then give mean reading equal to the true mean plus \(b\) and mean random error. Their difference is \(0.10d+c+e_1-e_2\). A shared offset cancels exactly from the contrast, whereas a differential offset is amplified tenfold when the contrast is reconstructed.

If the differential offset has an independently calibrated mean zero and known standard deviation \(s_c\), variance propagation gives \(\operatorname{Var}(\hat d)=(2s_e^2+s_c^2)/0.10^2\) for equal individual standard deviations \(s_e\). Treating the offset as random is appropriate for an ensemble of independently calibrated experiments with the stated calibration model. Within one installation it is a common unknown across all repeated exposures. If only a deterministic bound on \(c\) is justified, its effect is better reported as a bounded systematic displacement in addition to random sampling uncertainty.

Test the decision after averaging a hundred exposures

Suppose the original pair is observed one hundred independent times with the same differential calibration. The observed average difference remains 0.16 K, individual channel noise is 0.10 K per exposure, and calibration tests support \(|c|\le0.04\) K. The standard deviation of the averaged difference from independent channel noise alone is

\[\sqrt{2}\,0.10/10=0.01414\,\mathrm{K}.\]

In physical contrast units the random standard deviation is 0.1414 K. The possible systematic error is \(0.04/0.10=0.40\) K in either direction, undiminished by the additional exposures.

A conservative one-sided 95 percent lower limit under Gaussian independent channel noise subtracts both the worst allowed positive offset and the sampling margin. In contrast units it gives \(1.60-0.40-1.645(0.1414)\approx0.967\) K. Even with one hundred exposures this calculation falls slightly below the one-kelvin engineering threshold. Omitting the differential offset would give a lower limit about 1.367 K and reverse the decision. The discrepancy originates in the calibration model, although all raw measurements and all random-noise calculations are unchanged.

What new evidence would change the result? A reference specimen known to have zero contrast estimates \(c\) directly through the observed channel difference. If calibration tightens the guaranteed bound to 0.01 K, the same calculation gives \(1.60-0.10-0.233\approx1.267\) K. If the reference itself has uncertain contrast, propagate that uncertainty through its own blur factor before claiming the tighter bound. This comparison assigns the new measurement a clear purpose: it reduces the component of uncertainty aligned with the decision, rather than collecting more observations of a systematic offset that cancels in neither channel difference nor repeated averages.

An orientation-reversal test can also distinguish true contrast from a fixed differential channel offset. Reversing a specimen of equal geometry reverses the sign of its true contrast but preserves the channel offset. Half the difference of the two measured contrasts then isolates the blurred physical contrast, provided the specimen is stable and both orientations sample comparable regions.

Design the reversal and propagate its uncertainty

Write the normal and reversed orientation mean differences as \(D_+=0.10d+c+\epsilon_+\) and \(D_-=-0.10d+c+\epsilon_-\). Solving for the two unknowns gives

\[\hat d=(D_+-D_-)/0.20,\]
\[\hat c=(D_++D_-)/2.\]

The independent information comes from changing the sign of the physical response while keeping the offset fixed. Repeating the normal orientation alone produces another row proportional to the first and cannot separate the two quantities, however many times it is repeated.

Suppose each orientation is averaged over 100 exposures with the same independent channel noise as before. The standard deviation of either orientation difference is 0.01414 K. If the two averages have independent random errors, then \(\operatorname{sd}(\hat d)=\sqrt{2}(0.01414)/0.20=0.10\) K and \(\operatorname{sd}(\hat c)=0.010\) K. With readings 0.18 K and −0.14 K, one obtains \(d=1.60\) K and \(c=0.02\) K as estimates. The one-sided lower contrast limit is \(1.60-1.645(0.10)\approx1.435\) K. Unlike the earlier bounded-offset calculation, this inference estimates the shared differential offset from the design itself.

If the specimen cools differently between orientations, or rotating it changes the sensor footprint, the two signs might no longer describe equivalent regions. A reversal carried out in alternating order across independent blocks can expose drift. Check the recovered offsets for consistency across these blocks before claiming that orientation has separated the physical contrast from an instrument artifact.

Problem sheet 6 · Edges, decision thresholds and validation

Determine whether an apparent feature survives a change of penalty, a noise calculation and an independent test.

1. Fusing two adjacent estimates

Take identity observations \(b=(1,2.2)\). Minimise \(\frac12\sum(x_i-b_i)^2+\lambda|x_2-x_1|\). (a) Compute both fitted values for \(\lambda=0.2\) and 0.7. (b) Identify the fusion threshold. (c) Does observing a flat fit establish equal true values?

Retain the mean and shrink the half-difference

The observed mean is 1.6 and half-difference 0.6. At \(\lambda=0.2\), the fitted half-difference is 0.4 and the fit is \((1.2,2.0)\). At 0.7 it is zero, giving \((1.6,1.6)\). Fusion starts at \(\lambda=0.6\). A flat solution can be induced by the penalty; report the relevant contrast, noise and sensitivity to penalty strength before making a physical equality claim.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Inspect soft-thresholding and quadratic shrinkage. For two-cell total variation, apply the threshold to the half-difference and retain the mean as derived here.

2. A blur and a threshold

A sensor attenuates a physical contrast by 0.20 and gives a measured difference 0.60 K with standard deviation 0.20 K. (a) Find the data-only estimated contrast and standard deviation. (b) Can a one-sided 95% lower limit establish that the contrast exceeds 2 K? Use the Gaussian multiplier 1.645. (c) Assuming independent repeats with identical mean, how many would make that lower limit exceed 2 K?

Propagate the contrast scale only once

The estimate is 3 K with standard deviation 1 K. The lower limit \(3-1.645=1.355\) K is below 2 K. If the sample mean remains 3 K when expressed in physical units, require \(1.645/\sqrt N<1\), so three independent repeats suffice for that projected precision. Future means are random, hence this is a conditional planning calculation rather than a guaranteed outcome.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the corresponding calculation and compare its independently recomputed values with the equation derived in this solution.

3. Challenge a smooth reconstruction

A smoothed image fits all training readings within their stated noise and shows one broad transition. (a) Describe two distinct synthetic truths to use in a resolution audit. (b) Specify two numerical metrics and one measurement to hold out. (c) Explain how a code error, statistical uncertainty and regularisation bias would appear differently across the tests.

Use tests that can disagree with the interpretation

Choose a sharp step and a gradual ramp with similar large-scale contrast. Generate observations using a finer or independent forward discretisation, realistic noise and the actual sensor footprint. Record recovered edge location and recovered contrast or width. Reserve an independent measurement that is sensitive to the transition, ideally at a finer spatial scale. Failure on a noiseless manufactured case can expose an implementation mistake. Scatter across noise realisations quantifies sampling variation. A consistently broadened step across seeds at an otherwise verified discretisation indicates regularisation or finite sensor resolution; vary both separately to distinguish them.

Choose a sensor direction that resolves an unobservable combination

A prior for two parameters is Gaussian with zero mean and identity covariance. A first reading measures x₁+x₂ = 2.0 with noise standard deviation 0.1. Find the posterior mean and the variance along each normalised sum and difference direction. A second sensor of the same noise level can measure the sum again or the difference. Which design constrains both parameters, and why?

Worked solution

In the orthonormal directions s = (1,1)/√2 and d = (1,−1)/√2, the first measurement row is (√2,0). Its precision contribution has diagonal (200,0). Adding the unit prior precision gives posterior variances 1/201 and 1. Multiplying the precision-inverse by Aᵀb/0.01 gives a posterior mean with both components 200/201 ≈ 0.995.

Repeating the sum doubles the sum-direction information, giving variances 1/401 and 1. Measuring the difference instead adds information along d, giving 1/201 for both directions. The trace of the second covariance is about 0.00995, versus about 1.0025 for the repeated-sum design. The second measured value changes the posterior mean but, for this linear Gaussian model, not the covariance.

An objective concerned only with the sum would favour the repeated sensor. For reconstructing both parameters, sensitivity in independent directions matters. A very small residual for the original measurement is compatible with a whole line of plausible parameter pairs, which is why reporting only that residual would conceal the design difference.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the corresponding calculation and compare its independently recomputed values with the equation derived in this solution.

Chapter 13 · Choosing regularisation when the true answer is hidden

Derive two parameter choices from different information, then see how an excellent fit to sensor data can restore the noise in a weakly visible mode.

Each observation competes with a penalty

In orthogonal singular coordinates, let \(d_i=\sigma_i x_i+\eta_i\). For a candidate \(\lambda>0\), minimise \(\sum_i(\sigma_i x_i-d_i)^2+\lambda^2\sum_i x_i^2\). Setting each derivative to zero gives

\[\widehat x_i=\sigma_i d_i/(\sigma_i^2+\lambda^2).\]

Predicted data are \(H_i d_i\), where \(H_i=\sigma_i^2/(\sigma_i^2+\lambda^2)\). These factors range from zero to one. Their sum is the effective number of fitted data directions.

If an independently obtained bound for the whitened noise norm is \(\delta\), compare \(\sum_i[(1-H_i)d_i]^2\) with \(\delta^2\). The residual rises as \(\lambda\) increases. A discrepancy choice seeks the largest smoothing consistent with the stated noise level. If there is no crossing, either the search range, model or noise estimate needs examination.

A prediction criterion without a noise bound

For a linear smoother \(\widehat d=Hd\), deleting observation \(i\) gives the leave-one-out residual \(r_i/(1-H_{ii})\) under the usual fixed-design ridge fit. Generalised cross-validation replaces the individual leverages by their mean. This produces the computable quantity

\[\mathrm{GCV}(\lambda)=\frac{\|d-\widehat d\|^2/n}{[1-\operatorname{trace}(H)/n]^2}.\]

The denominator penalises fits that spend nearly one degree of freedom per datum. The criterion estimates predictive performance under assumptions on noise and design. A selected value need not minimise the error in the unknown parameter, and highly unequal leverages can defeat the approximation.

A sensor that barely sees a mode

Take sensitivities \(1,0.01\), true parameters \((1,1)\) and data \((1,0.02)\). Unregularised inversion returns \((1,2)\) with zero residual. A perturbation of one hundredth in the second observation doubled the second parameter. With \(\lambda=0.01\) the second gain is \(\sigma/(\sigma^2+\lambda^2)=50\), so the same datum yields 1. This exact agreement with the hidden truth is accidental.

For \(\lambda=0.1\), the second estimate drops to about 0.0198. A weak direction has been almost removed. Meanwhile the first estimate remains about 0.9901. A single residual norm does not tell you which parameter direction was suppressed.

Inspect an entire regularisation path

Three synthetic sensors have sensitivities \(1,0.2,0.02\), true parameters all equal to one, and an adjustable perturbation only in the last sensor. This deliberately uneven noise makes it possible to test when the GCV assumptions are plausible. Inspect parameter error and data residual over a logarithmic range of \(\lambda\). The result reports the best GCV value on the sampled grid and the residual at that choice.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on choosing a criterion

If all \(H_i\) equal a common \(h<1\), can GCV distinguish different values of \(h\) for fixed data?

Worked solution

The numerator contains \((1-h)^2\|d\|^2/n\) and the denominator \((1-h)^2\). They cancel exactly. There is no identifiable minimum from this score.

If residuals remain much larger than a well-calibrated noise norm even at negligible regularisation, should increasing the penalty help?

Worked solution

Increasing the penalty shrinks the fit and usually increases residual. Check the forward model, its range and data preparation, including the uncertainty units used in whitening.

Read the university treatment

Compare the information each stopping rule needs about measurement noise with the information available in the three-sensor experiment. Per Christian Hansen · Stopping rules for noisy data

Study the full derivation

Choose a regularisation scale by predicting omitted data · Inferring parameters and fields from observations

Regularised least squares can fit each observation partly with itself. Derive leave-one-out residuals from a single factorisation, approximate them by a trace and identify when a generalised cross-validation score misses a highly influential sensor.

Chapter 14 · Learning a drifting temperature from a sequence of readings

Derive the scalar Kalman update by completing the square and see how process uncertainty controls memory of the past.

Propagate a distribution through time

A drifting offset follows \(x_k=x_{k-1}+w_k\). A thermometer reports \(z_k=x_k+v_k\). Assume independent, zero-mean Gaussian process increments of variance \(Q\) and sensor noise of variance \(R\), independent at each time and of the initial state. Before the next reading, the mean stays at \(m^-=m_{k-1}\) and the variance adds, \(P^-=P_{k-1}+Q\).

The negative log posterior, up to constants, is \((x-m^-)^2/(2P^-)+(z_k-x)^2/(2R)\). Collect the coefficient of \(x^2\) and its linear term. The result can be written in two equivalent forms:

\[P_k=(1/P^-+1/R)^{-1},\]
\[m_k=m^-+K(z_k-m^-),\]
\[K=P^-/(P^-+R).\]

Thus the fraction of the innovation used is a model prediction. When sensors are noisy the gain falls. When the model permits rapid drift, uncertainty grows between readings, giving the new measurement a larger role. These are variances, so a stated standard deviation must be squared first.

A reading with units and a repeated reading

A prior says \(20.0\pm2.0\) °C (one standard deviation), with negligible intervening drift. A sensor of standard deviation 1 °C reads 23 °C. The gain is \(4/(4+1)=0.8\), giving \(22.4\) °C and variance \(0.8\) °C². A second independent reading of 23 °C gives gain \(0.8/1.8\) and mean \(22.6667\) °C. Treating a duplicate record as independent would falsely claim the same information gain.

In a long random walk with \(Q=0.25\) and \(R=1\) in squared units, a steady posterior variance satisfies

\[P=(P+Q)R/(P+Q+R).\]

Rearrangement gives \(P^2+QP-QR=0\), so the physically positive root is about 0.3904. New uncertainty replenishes the information squeezed out by measurement.

Follow a change in the underlying temperature

For a reproducible illustration, a fixed synthetic thermometer trace contains a smooth periodic disturbance and a step in the hidden temperature. It is deliberately held fixed while the filter's assumed \(Q\) and \(R\) change. Compare the lag just after the step with the wiggle once the temperature settles.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on noisy data over time

If \(Q=0\), show how \(n\) independent readings with the same variance affect precision.

Worked solution

Each update adds \(1/R\) to precision \(1/P\). Induction gives

\[1/P_n=1/P_0+n/R.\]

This is precisely the precision of a batch Gaussian update.

What pattern in the innovations would call the random-walk assumptions into question?

Worked solution

A long run of positive innovations after a systematic ramp suggests bias in the prediction. Serial correlation suggests missing dynamics or correlated observation errors. Check against predicted innovation variance \(P^-+R\) and examine signs and lags as well as their sizes.

Read the university treatment

Extend the scalar precision update to several state variables and identify the matrix made invertible by a positive observation variance. Stephen Boyd · Stanford EE363, estimation and the Kalman filter

What each new sensor reading changes in a Gaussian inverse problem · Inferring parameters and fields from observations

Start with an uncertain vector, condition on one noisy linear observation, and reconstruct the posterior mean and covariance without repeating a batch inversion. Check shared calibration errors before treating repeated measurements as independent evidence.

Chapter 15 · When a single uncertainty interval hides two explanations

Start from a squared-amplitude measurement, derive the full posterior and design a second observation capable of identifying the sign.

Symmetry is an identifiability statement

A sensor measures \(d=x^2+\eta\) with Gaussian noise of standard deviation \(s\). A symmetric Gaussian prior of variance \(\tau^2\) puts equal weight on opposite signs. Bayes' rule gives the density

\[p(x\mid d)\propto\exp\!\left[-\frac{(d-x^2)^2}{2s^2}-\frac{x^2}{2\tau^2}\right].\]

The exponent is even, so the posterior mean is exactly zero whenever the integral exists. That mean may make an extremely poor prediction of the observed quantity. To locate modes, differentiate the negative log density to get \(x[2(x^2-d)/s^2+1/\tau^2]\). Two off-centre modes appear precisely when \(d>s^2/(2\tau^2)\). At smaller \(d\), the prior and noise merge the explanations into one central mode.

Numbers and competing summaries

Set \(d=1\), \(s=0.2\), and \(\tau=2\). The modes are at \(\pm\sqrt{0.995}\), close to \(\pm0.9975\). The density ratio of \(x=0\) to \(x=1\) is \(\exp[-1/(2s^2)+1/(2\tau^2)]\), about \(4.2\times10^{-6}\). The reported mean of zero sits in a very low-density region.

For \(d=-0.05\), there is no real parameter that exactly fits the measured amplitude squared. A noisy sensor can still produce this value. The distribution has a single mode at zero, and there is no justification for taking the square root of the negative datum or discarding the observation.

Buy a measurement that identifies the sign

Assume an independent second sensor reports \(b=x+\epsilon\) with known standard deviation 0.5. Add \(-(b-x)^2/(2\cdot0.5^2)\) to the log density. The original two signs now receive different weights. Both curves in the plot are independently normalised by quadrature on the displayed interval; the sign probability is conditional on that interval. Compare the observation \(b=0\) with \(b=1\).

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on designing another experiment

Repeat the squared-amplitude measurement a hundred times with independent noise. What can become precise?

Worked solution

The magnitude \(x^2\) can become very precise. Every likelihood factor remains even in \(x\), so the sign probabilities stay equal with this prior. More data of the same kind cannot break that symmetry.

If the two candidate states were exactly \(x=\pm1\), what likelihood ratio does \(b=0.5\) give in favour of the positive state?

Worked solution

The exponent difference is

\[((0.5+1)^2-(0.5-1)^2)/(2\cdot0.5^2)=4.\]

The likelihood ratio is \(e^4\), about 54.6. A continuous posterior also averages over the spread around each candidate.

Chapter 16 · How model error masquerades as a fitted physical parameter

Project an omitted spatial effect into the fitted parameter space. Work out when residual checks reveal the omission and when they are silent.

A misspecified instrument

Suppose \(d=A x_*+b+\eta\), with zero-mean measurement noise and a deterministic but omitted contribution \(b\). Assume \(A\) has independent columns. Least squares gives

\[\widehat x=(A^TA)^{-1}A^Td.\]

Taking expectations leaves the parameter bias \(A^+b\). The fitted residual is \((I-AA^+)(b+\eta)\). Thus the same model error separates into a component that biases the parameters and an orthogonal component that remains visible to residual checks.

For a single sensitivity column \(a\), decompose \(b=b_\parallel+b_\perp\), where \(b_\parallel=a(a^Tb)/(a^Ta)\). Repeating the measurement reduces independent random error; the deterministic projection remains. Error bars computed solely from measurement variance can become more misleading as more readings are acquired.

Calibration with an omitted offset

Fit \(d_i=m t_i\) at \(t=(1,2,3)\) to observations actually generated by \(d_i=2t_i+0.3\). The unmodelled constant has nonzero projection onto \(t\). The bias is \(0.3(1+2+3)/(1+4+9)=0.12857\), giving fitted slope 2.12857. The residuals at the three points are approximately 0.17143, 0.04286 and −0.08571. Their signed trend suggests a missing intercept. At \(t=6\) the model predicts 12.7714 while the actual law gives 12.3; an apparently modest training residual has grown into a substantial extrapolation error.

Now use a discrepancy exactly proportional to time, \(b_i=0.1t_i\). The fitted slope becomes 2.1 with zero residual at every training point. Additional times under the same model cannot separate the true slope from this contribution. A separate calibration that suppresses the extra physical effect can.

Rotate the missing physics

In a two-reading instrument, the model sensitivity is the unit horizontal vector. Vary the magnitude and direction of the omitted contribution. Observe parameter bias and visible residual separately, and locate cases with the same discrepancy norm but completely different fit diagnoses.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on experimental design

Repeat the offset example at symmetric times \((-1,0,1)\). Which part of the fitted result changes?

Worked solution

The constant is now orthogonal to \(t\), giving zero slope bias. The residual is a constant 0.3 in every reading. The experiment has made an offset visible without corrupting the slope.

Can adding an unrestricted discrepancy parameter for every datum solve the identifiability problem?

Worked solution

It permits exact fits for any physical parameter by assigning the remaining signal to the discrepancy. Constrain the discrepancy from theory, independent data or a justified prior to extract the physical parameter.

Read the university treatment

Compare errors that project into a fitted singular direction with errors seen in the orthogonal data residual. Per Christian Hansen · Discrete Inverse Problems

Chapter 17 · Predicting a new measurement when the fitted parameters are correlated

Propagate the full posterior covariance to a quantity of interest, distinguish latent and measured uncertainty and select the observation that reduces the uncertainty of the actual prediction.

One model supplies many possible targets

For parameters \(\theta\) with posterior mean \(m\) and covariance \(C\), a linear target \(s=h^T\theta\) has mean \(h^Tm\). Subtract the mean and expand the quadratic product to obtain

\[\operatorname{Var}(s)=h^TCh.\]

An independent sensor with variance \(R\) changes the variance of a future measured value to \(h^TCh+R\). Repeated fits of noisy datasets and repeated physical measurements are distinct sources of variation.

A candidate new observation \(z=a^T\theta+\epsilon\) with variance \(R_a\) has covariance \(h^TCa\) with the target. For a Gaussian posterior the conditional covariance update is \(C_+=C-Caa^TC/(a^TCa+R_a)\), found either by completing the square or projecting out the optimal multiple of the innovation. The target variance decreases by

\[\Delta\operatorname{Var}(s)=\frac{(h^TCa)^2}{a^TCa+R_a}.\]
Background · Posterior predictive
\[p(y_*|y)=\int p(y_*|m)p(m|y)\,dm\]

A distribution over parameters induces uncertainty in a new observation. Measurement noise and uncertainty of the prediction mean are distinct contributions.

A precise sum and an uncertain difference

Let \(C\) have diagonal entries 1 and correlation \(-0.9\). A target \(h=(1,1)\) has variance \(2-1.8=0.2\). The difference \(h=(1,-1)\) has variance 3.8. Ignoring correlations would predict 2 for both. With sensor variance 0.25, a newly measured sum has variance 0.45 although the latent sum variance remains 0.2.

For the target sum, measuring the first coordinate with variance 0.25 reduces variance by \((1-0.9)^2/(1+0.25)=0.008\). Measuring the sum itself at the same variance reduces it by \(0.2^2/(0.2+0.25)=0.0889\). These two sensors have differently normalised sensitivity vectors. Their stated noise levels refer to their own output units and must be calibrated before a cost comparison.

Choose the measurement direction

For a fairer geometric comparison, restrict both measurement and prediction directions to unit length. Fix the target along the normalised sum. Sweep candidate observation angles and read the remaining prediction variance. Change the parameter correlation: when does a coordinate sensor improve the target appreciably?

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on prediction and parameter recovery

If \(h^TCa=0\), can a noise-free measurement of \(a^T\theta\) improve the linear prediction under these Gaussian assumptions?

Worked solution

The covariance reduction is exactly zero. The measurement may determine another direction precisely while carrying no linear information about the target.

The posterior on \(\theta\) is strongly non-Gaussian. Which conclusions still hold exactly?

Worked solution

Mean and variance of a fixed linear prediction still follow from its first two moments, and independent sensor variance still adds. The Gaussian conditional update and an interval based only on two standard deviations need additional assumptions.

Read the university treatment

Compare the projection of a vector measurement with the covariance of a scalar target. Check the direction of each matrix product. Stephen Boyd · Stanford EE363, estimation

Chapter 18 · Calibrating two instruments when both axes fluctuate

The horizontal axis of a calibration plot is often described as an input. In this experiment it is itself a reading from a second noisy instrument. Infer a straight calibration relation from paired data, derive the metric in which the fit should be performed, and use a change of units to reveal an easily overlooked modelling error.

What was actually held fixed during the experiment?

At setting \(i\), let the unknown true first-channel signal be \(\xi_i\), measured in millivolts. Suppose the true second-channel value, in kelvin, is \(a+b\xi_i\) over the limited operating range. What the laboratory records is the pair \(x_i=\xi_i+\epsilon_{xi}\),

\[y_i=a+b\xi_i+\epsilon_{yi}.\]

Repeatability tests supply standard deviations \(s_x,s_y>0\). For now fix them across observations, with independent zero-mean Gaussian errors in both channels. The physical settings \(\xi_i\) are treated as unknown fixed quantities, one per record.

Ordinary vertical least squares implicitly treats the observed \(x_i\) as the exact horizontal values. That choice would make sense if the settings had been specified by a sufficiently accurate standard and \(x_i\) denoted those settings. It changes the inference when \(x_i\) comes from a noisy meter. Every displayed point can then move both horizontally and vertically as we estimate its underlying position on the calibration line.

A geometric phrase like “shortest distance to the line” has no unique experimental meaning yet. A displacement of one millivolt and a displacement of one kelvin cannot be compared by drawing the points on square graph paper. Measurement uncertainty supplies a metric. Divide each horizontal adjustment by \(s_x\) and each vertical one by \(s_y\). Under the stated Gaussian assumptions their squared sum is proportional to twice the negative log likelihood, up to additive constants independent of the parameters. This also clarifies why the relative scale requires a separate calibration: one cloud does not automatically reveal how much of its spread belongs to each axis.

Eliminate the unobserved setting algebraically

For a proposed line, estimate each unknown \(\xi_i\) by minimising its pair of squared corrections. Define \(r_i=y_i-a-bx_i\) and write \(\xi_i=x_i+\delta_i\). Then the horizontal term is \(\delta_i^2/s_x^2\), and the vertical term is \((r_i-b\delta_i)^2/s_y^2\). Differentiating their sum with respect to \(\delta_i\) leaves a scalar linear equation. Its solution apportions the original vertical residual between the two measurement errors.

\[\delta_i=\frac{b s_x^2 r_i}{s_y^2+b^2s_x^2},\]
\[y_i-a-b\xi_i=\frac{s_y^2r_i}{s_y^2+b^2s_x^2}.\]

For example, let \(b=2\,\mathrm{K/mV}\), \(s_x=0.5\,\mathrm{mV}\), \(s_y=1\,\mathrm K\), and \(r_i=1\,\mathrm K\). The inferred horizontal correction is 0.25 mV and the remaining vertical error is 0.5 K. In uncertainty units each has magnitude one half and the squared cost is one half. Keeping the first reading fixed would assign the whole kelvin to the second channel and produce cost one. The formula decides the split from the line sensitivity and the two instrument uncertainties.

Substitute the optimal correction into the objective. The profiled contribution simplifies to \(r_i^2/(s_y^2+b^2s_x^2)\). For fixed slope the denominator is common to all records. Differentiating with respect to \(a\) therefore gives

\[a=\bar y-b\bar x.\]

Every candidate slope now passes through the sample centroid. The remaining scalar optimisation is an independent way to check a geometric solution.

\[J(b)=\frac{\sum_i[(y_i-\bar y)-b(x_i-\bar x)]^2}{s_y^2+b^2s_x^2}.\]

Do not attach a slope-dependent logarithm of this denominator to the present objective. We obtained it by maximising over fixed nuisance coordinates, each with its own original pair of measurement densities and fixed noise normalisation. A different experiment might specify a distribution for the latent settings and integrate them out instead. That would be a different likelihood construction. The fitting rule follows from how the unknowns and observations have been modelled.

Fit in coordinates in which the noise is isotropic

Make a data matrix with one row per observation. Subtract the centroid and divide the first column by \(s_x\), the second by \(s_y\). Call the result \(Z\). A line through the origin in these whitened coordinates is completely specified by a unit normal \(n\), including vertical lines that would cause a slope parameter to diverge. The squared perpendicular distances sum to \(\|Zn\|^2=n^TZ^TZn\). Minimising over unit normals selects the eigenvector of the smallest eigenvalue of \(Z^TZ\). With a singular value decomposition \(Z=U\Sigma V^T\), it is the last right singular vector.

The other right singular vector points along the fitted line. Transform that direction back to the physical instrument units by multiplying its components by \(s_x\) and \(s_y\). The slope is the ratio of those transformed components. The sign of the singular vector is mathematically arbitrary and has no effect on the line. The feet of the perpendiculars are simple in whitened coordinates: subtract \((z_i\cdot n)n\) from each whitened point. Transform them back and add the centroid. In a physical plot with unlike axis scales those connecting segments need not look perpendicular by eye.

A three-point calibration you can check without a computer

Use dimensionless readings \((-1,-2),(0,1),(1,1)\) and equal measurement standard deviations. Both column means are zero. The centred sums are

\[S_{xx}=2,S_{yy}=6,S_{xy}=3.\]

Ordinary vertical least squares gives slope \(3/2\). For a line direction \((1,b)\), requiring that this direction be an eigenvector of the scatter matrix yields

\[3+6b=b(2+3b).\]

There are two roots. The direction along the major axis corresponds to the positive one,

\[b=(4+\sqrt{52})/6\simeq1.8685.\]

The negative root is the perpendicular minor-axis direction.

The residual cost of the vertical fit, evaluated in its own vertical metric, should not be directly compared with the perpendicular cost of the other fit. Evaluate both using the same profiled cost instead. With \(s_x=s_y=1\), the minimum equals the smaller scatter eigenvalue \((8-\sqrt{52})/2\simeq0.39445\). Substituting 1.5 into the profiled formula yields \((6-9+4.5)/(1+2.25)\simeq0.46154\). The orthogonal fit has achieved the smaller value in the metric actually specified by the instrument model.

When is a best direction too weak to interpret?

The separating quantity is the gap between the two eigenvalues of the whitened scatter matrix. If they are nearly equal, the data cloud is nearly round in the metric that matters. Rotating the line then changes the score very little. If they are exactly equal, every line through the centre has the same score. A numerical routine can return a perfectly normalised eigenvector in this situation. Its normalisation and tiny algebraic residual say nothing about whether the data determine a preferred direction.

Try the notebook's seven paired readings first with its default uncertainty scales. The weighted slope is approximately 1.7290 K/mV and the vertical slope about 1.6490 K/mV. Decrease the declared uncertainty of the first channel while retaining the numerical observations. The fit approaches the vertical least-squares answer because horizontal adjustments become expensive. Increasing this uncertainty instead assigns more of a discrepancy to the first instrument. When the recorded inputs span too narrow a range, even very accurate readouts cannot robustly establish a sensitivity over a wide operating range.

A reliable implementation should survive changes of units. Convert millivolts to volts, and divide both the first coordinates and their standard deviation by one thousand. The whitened first column and the minimum score remain unchanged. The newly reported slope has units of kelvin per volt and is one thousand times the original physical number. If the slope fails this conversion test, inspect noise scaling, plotting aspect ratios and any normalisation by sample variance that was used in place of a known measurement uncertainty.

One may also ask for a confidence interval on the inferred line. That requires a sampling model for repeat experiments. In particular, treating each corrected pair as a known physical truth ignores uncertainty in the inferred settings. A simple exploratory test simulates new independent measurement errors at plausible fitted settings, refits the complete procedure and examines the spread of slopes over many trials. The result is conditional on those settings and the stated noise levels. A common calibration error shared by every specimen or correlated errors within a reading would require a different covariance model before interpreting such an interval.

A useful practical control is to interchange the two instruments, fit again with interchanged uncertainties, and convert the line to the original orientation. Whitened orthogonal regression should recover the same geometry. In contrast, two separate vertical regressions generally give different lines after their slopes are inverted. Directional regression can be an appropriate predictor for new observations under another sampling model; this reversal test specifically examines the symmetry of the latent-line calibration adopted here.

Tutorial on calibration uncertainty and identifiability

1. With slope 2 and a positive vertical residual of 1 in the units of the hand example, change \(s_x\) to 0.25 and retain \(s_y=1\). What correction is assigned to each instrument?

Work through the solution

The denominator becomes

\[1+4(0.25)^2=1.25.\]

The latent first coordinate increases by \(2(0.25)^2/1.25=0.1\). The unexplained vertical error is

\[1/1.25=0.8.\]

Scaled corrections of 0.4 and 0.8 give a total cost of 0.80. Improving the horizontal meter shifts more of the inferred error to the vertical channel.

2. Multiply both standard deviations by three while retaining the same actual data. Which reported quantities change?

Work through the solution

The centred whitened matrix is divided by three. Both singular values scale by one third, but the right singular directions and fitted physical line stay fixed. The squared score decreases by a factor of nine. A fixed fit can therefore be more or less consistent with an instrument specification even when its slope is unchanged.

3. Four measurements in whitened coordinates lie at \((1,0),(-1,0),(0,1),(0,-1)\). What slope will a physically meaningful calibration report?

Work through the solution

The scatter matrix is twice the identity, so every line through the origin has squared score two. A unique calibration slope cannot be recovered from these measurements under the stated equal-uncertainty model. To break the symmetry, obtain additional measurements spanning a wider range of true settings or supply new information about instrument uncertainties.

Problem sheet 7 · Project · Which thermometer should the laboratory buy?

Separate an unknown common temperature, a calibration contrast and temporal drift. Design a measurement that improves the weakly determined quantity and check whether a small residual signals a trustworthy reconstruction.

Resolve two independent directions

Two calibrated outputs in orthogonal data coordinates are \(d_1=x_1+\eta_1\), \(d_2=0.02x_2+\eta_2\). Both noise standard deviations are 0.01 °C. The observed outputs are 1.00 and 0.03. Compute the unregularised estimates and their standard deviations. Which parameter does the experiment measure reliably?

Work through the solution

The estimates are 1 and 1.5. Independent noise standard deviations propagate by division by the sensitivities, giving 0.01 and 0.5. Both fitted data residuals are zero. Sensor fit alone has hidden the factor of fifty between parameter uncertainties.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the corresponding calculation and compare its independently recomputed values with the equation derived in this solution.

Separate an assumption from a measurement

With \(\lambda=0.02\) apply the zero-centred Tikhonov penalty. Calculate both fitted parameters, their predicted data and the residual. Compare with the regularisation path. Its three-channel test adds a middle sensitivity of 0.2. Set the weak-channel error to 0.010 for the same weak observation and compare the weak-channel prediction. The third sensor changes the total residual norm. Would another identical weak measurement halve the unregularised standard deviation?

Work through the solution

The first estimate is \(1/1.0004\approx0.9996\). The second gain is \(0.02/(0.0004+0.0004)=25\), hence \(x_2=0.75\). The two predicted data are about 0.9996 and 0.015. Subtracting these from the observed data gives residuals 0.0004 and 0.015, with Euclidean norm about 0.0150. Two independent readings at the same design divide standard deviation by \(\sqrt 2\). A change of sensitivity from 0.02 to 0.04 would halve it directly.

Reuse the sensors over time

For a common temperature whose prior variance is 0.04 °C², a time step adds process variance 0.01 °C². A new independent reading has variance 0.04 °C² and lies 0.3 °C above prediction. Calculate the posterior mean shift and standard deviation. Use the temperature filter to predict what increasing process variance does to lag.

Work through the solution

The predicted variance is 0.05. Gain is \(0.05/(0.05+0.04)=5/9\), hence the mean shifts by 0.1667 °C. The posterior variance is \(0.05(4/9)=0.02222\), giving standard deviation 0.1491 °C. Larger process variance gives a higher gain, tending to reduce lag while allowing more measurement variability into the estimate.

Audit the purchase decision

A cheaper replacement responds to \(x_2^2\) with very low noise. Is it a good replacement when the sign of the calibration contrast decides whether to add or subtract a correction? Explore the signed measurement experiment.

Work through the solution

A precise squared measurement alone still cannot distinguish equal positive and negative contrasts under a symmetric prior. A weak signed reading can split the two explanations. Evaluate loss in the corrected temperature and the cost of choosing the wrong sign before choosing hardware.

Problem sheet 8 · Estimate optical thickness when both measurements count photons

A dim red calibration source is viewed through an absorbing filter and then with the filter removed. Both observations fluctuate. Build the inverse problem directly from the photon counts, profile out the unknown source brightness, and decide how to distribute scarce observation time.

Choose the generative model before transforming the data

Take a linear counter with negligible dark counts and a stable monochromatic source. In a blank exposure of duration \(t_b\) the photon rate is an unknown \(\lambda\); with the sample inserted the rate is \(\lambda e^{-a}\), where optical thickness \(a=\mu L\ge0\) is the desired parameter. Counts \(b\) and \(s\) in disjoint exposure windows are independent Poisson random variables. At only a few photons it is quite possible to record zero. Consider a glass sample of thickness 2.00 mm and ask which part of the inference requires this geometric information.

Profile out the source rate

Write the joint log likelihood at finite counts, then find the best source brightness for each proposed optical thickness.

Work through the solution

Independence adds the log probabilities. Ignoring only terms independent of both unknown parameters gives

\[\ell(\lambda,a)=(b+s)\ln\lambda-sa-\lambda(t_b+t_s e^{-a})+\mathrm{constant}.\]

Holding \(a\) fixed, set \(\partial_\lambda\ell=0\) to obtain \(\hat\lambda(a)=(b+s)/(t_b+t_se^{-a})\) whenever the total count is positive. Substitution gives

\[\ell_p(a)=-sa-(b+s)\ln(t_b+t_se^{-a})+\mathrm{constant}.\]

The specimen thickness \(L\) has yet to enter the calculation. One measures optical thickness directly; reporting an absorption coefficient \(\mu=a/L\) additionally assumes a known geometrical length and an appropriate single-pass transmission model. Scattering out of the acceptance or reflections can contribute to apparent extinction if the apparatus admits them.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the experiment on this same project page and compare its output with this part of the derivation.

Check the result with an independent probability model

Condition on the observed total count

Show that the sample count given total \(n=b+s\) is binomial. Derive the interior maximum and evaluate it for equal exposure times with \(b=40,\ s=10\).

Work through the solution

Conditional on a recorded photon belonging to either run, its probability of being in the sample run is \(p(a)=t_s e^{-a}/(t_b+t_s e^{-a})\). The binomial probability of observing \(s\) such assignments among \(n\) is proportional to \(p^s(1-p)^b\). Substitution reproduces the same profile likelihood as eliminating brightness analytically. The two routes make the disappearance of the source rate transparent. This conditioning removes its information about brightness while keeping the information about relative attenuation when brightness itself is free.

For positive interior counts the best fraction is \(s/n\), giving \(\hat a=\ln[(b/t_b)/(s/t_s)]\). With equal times it is \(\ln4=1.3863\), and the reported coefficient would be \(693.1\ \mathrm{m^{-1}}\). If the sample rate happens to exceed the blank rate, the constrained maximum is \(a=0\). A negative unconstrained estimate is still informative as a check on fluctuations or alignment. Hiding it with a silent positivity correction makes repeated calibrations difficult to interpret.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the experiment on this same project page and compare its output with this part of the derivation.

Ask what the experiment establishes when no photon arrives

Contrast a curvature estimate with an exact one-sided statement

Evaluate the local standard error for the positive-count example. For a second equal-time experiment with \(b=8,\ s=0\), obtain a one-sided conditional 95% lower bound on optical thickness.

Work through the solution

The profile curvature at the interior maximum has magnitude \(np(1-p)=bs/(b+s)\). Its reciprocal gives the local variance \(1/b+1/s\), so the standard error is \(\sqrt{0.125}=0.354\) for the first run. This local quadratic approximation is a high-count diagnostic. It is weak near a boundary and can miss asymmetry. A direct profile plot of twice the log likelihood loss reveals that asymmetry without taking the logarithm of an observed zero.

With zero sample counts and eight total, the MLE tends to infinity. Yet the data carry finite information. For a candidate \(p\), the conditional probability of observing zero is \((1-p)^8\). Solve \((1-p_U)^8=0.05\) to obtain

\[p_U=1-0.05^{1/8}=0.3123.\]

At equal exposure \(a=\ln[(1-p)/p]\), giving the exact one-sided conditional lower bound \(a\ge0.789\) after rounding. There is no finite upper bound from these counts alone. Adding one artificial count to numerator and denominator would manufacture a finite point estimate tied to that convention. A specified prior or a measured background can change the inference, but must be included in the generative model.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the experiment on this same project page and compare its output with this part of the derivation.

Allocate the two exposures and interpret the profile graph

For a total time \(T=t_b+t_s\) and a provisional transmission \(q=e^{-a}\), use expected counts to approximate \(\operatorname{var}(\hat a)\simeq1/(\lambda t_b)+1/(\lambda q t_s)\). Optimisation requires a provisional attenuation. After a short pilot run its uncertainty can be propagated through a range of candidate designs. The graph below instead holds observed counts fixed and recomputes the profile loss over trial optical thickness. Use its time-ratio slider to correct the transmission for unequal known exposures. It is a reanalysis of those counts under the selected acquisition timing, rather than a simulated independent observation.

Open this experiment online

Find the best time ratio and distinguish design from estimation

If \(q=1/4\), find the variance-minimising fraction of time spent on the specimen and compare with the strategy of aiming for equal expected counts. How would a fluctuating source alter the protocol?

Work through the solution

Substituting \(t_b=T-t_s\) and differentiating gives \((T-t_s)^{-2}=1/(q t_s^2)\), hence

\[t_s/t_b=1/\sqrt q=2.\]

Spend two thirds of the time on the specimen. The equal-count rule would require a ratio of four, overusing sample time while leaving the blank reference noisy. For \(\lambda T=120\), the optimum expected counts are 40 blank and 20 sample; the predicted variance is 0.075. At equal expected counts it becomes

\[2/24=0.0833.\]

The pilot estimate of transmission affects the best allocation but has no role in the profile formula once exposure times are fixed.

If source brightness varies systematically between the two runs, a lower sample rate can represent source drift rather than attenuation. Short interleaved blank and sample segments, or a simultaneously monitored reference, provide observations that identify this nuisance effect. More photons within a pair of separately drifting runs reduce counting error while leaving the confounding intact. At appreciable independently measured background, replace each Poisson mean by signal plus background and fit the counts jointly; the simple conditional probability above then also depends on the background rates.

Notebook practice · Calibrating two instruments when both axes fluctuate

Inferring parameters and fields from observations

Follow information from a forward model back to its unknowns. Evaluate identifiability, numerical sensitivity and how an additional observation changes the inference.

  1. Eliminate linear nuisance parameters before fitting a decay

    A measured relaxation has an unknown rate, amplitude and background. Derive a one-dimensional profile fit without fixing the nuisance parameters, explain why short observation windows can hide the rate and test an actual numerical dataset.

  2. Choose a regularisation scale by predicting omitted data

    Regularised least squares can fit each observation partly with itself. Derive leave-one-out residuals from a single factorisation, approximate them by a trace and identify when a generalised cross-validation score misses a highly influential sensor.

  3. Recovering sparse coefficients without hiding amplitude bias

    A sensor records a linear mixture of a few active components. Derive the soft-threshold map, use it in an iterative reconstruction and decide when correlated response columns make an apparently sharp sparse answer ambiguous.

  4. What each new sensor reading changes in a Gaussian inverse problem

    Start with an uncertain vector, condition on one noisy linear observation, and reconstruct the posterior mean and covariance without repeating a batch inversion. Check shared calibration errors before treating repeated measurements as independent evidence.

  5. What can unfinished endurance tests reveal about lifetime?

    When a test stops, a surviving bearing has supplied information. Derive the likelihood from first principles and examine how changing the observation deadline moves the rate inferred from a small batch.

  6. How an integral instrument becomes a matrix

    Construct two discrete models of the same measurement, diagnose noise with a Picard plot and test a regularisation parameter against a deliberately imperfect instrument model.

  7. Early stopping and penalties that leave part of a field free

    Derive two matrix-free least-squares iterations, find where noise first enters their solution and separate the measured and penalised directions of a nonstandard Tikhonov problem.

Inferring parameters and fields from observations · 1. Eliminate linear nuisance parameters before fitting a decay

A measured relaxation has an unknown rate, amplitude and background. Derive a one-dimensional profile fit without fixing the nuisance parameters, explain why short observation windows can hide the rate and test an actual numerical dataset.

Separate the unknowns by how they enter the observations

Suppose observations at known times \(t_i\) have mean \(y_i=b+a e^{-k t_i}\) with \(k>0\). Both \(a\) and \(b\) enter linearly once \(k\) is specified. For independent equal-variance measurement noise, minimising squared residuals is a natural fit. More general known covariance can be incorporated by whitening the data and model columns together. Fitting all three unknowns by nonlinear iteration is possible, but every candidate rate should be judged against the best available amplitude and background at that rate. Otherwise an apparent improvement or failure of the rate may merely reflect a poor nuisance estimate.

Let \(s_i=e^{-kt_i}\), bars denote sample means, and regress \(y_i\) on \(1,s_i\). Differentiating the sum of squared residuals with respect to \(b\) gives

\[b=\bar y-a\bar s.\]

Substitution and differentiation with respect to \(a\) yield

\[a(k)=\frac{\sum_i(s_i-\bar s)(y_i-\bar y)}{\sum_i(s_i-\bar s)^2},\]
\[b(k)=\bar y-a(k)\bar s .\]

The denominator vanishes when all times coincide or \(k=0\), at which point the two columns are identical. A QR or SVD of the whitened design reveals this rank loss. The simple centred formulas are transparent for analysis; for large poorly scaled datasets a stable factorisation remains preferable to unmonitored normal equations. Define profile objective \(\Phi(k)=\tfrac12\sum_i r_i^2\),

\[r_i=y_i-b(k)-a(k)s_i.\]

A one-dimensional search can now explore its shape, bounds or competing minima.

Differentiate the profiled objective without differentiating the whole fit

Write \(X=[\mathbf1,s]\), \(c=(b,a)^T\) and \(r=y-Xc\). At the least-squares solution \(X^Tr=0\). Taking the total derivative of \(\Phi=r^Tr/2\) gives \(-r^T(X'c+Xc')\). The term with \(c'\) vanishes by orthogonality, leaving \(\Phi'=-r^TX'c=a\sum_i r_i t_i e^{-kt_i}\). This simplification applies to the scalar objective gradient, not necessarily to the full residual Jacobian or second derivative. The projected component \((I-P_X)X'c\) is the local model change with rate that cannot be absorbed by a linear-nuisance adjustment. Its whitened squared norm characterises rate information near a well-fitting model.

At short times,

\[b+a e^{-kt}\simeq(a+b)-ak t+ak^2t^2/2+\cdots.\]

Data resolving only a constant and a slope constrain \(a+b\) and \(ak\), not \(a,b,k\) individually. Making the inner least-squares solve arbitrarily accurate cannot create curvature information absent from the acquisition. Include times approaching the decay time and a late observation near the baseline if feasible. Alternatively supply an independently characterised background with uncertainty rather than fixing it silently. A flat profile exposes this identifiability issue more clearly than a single successful three-parameter optimiser status.

Recover a rate exactly before introducing noise

At \(t=0,1,2,3\) s consider the noiseless data \(5,3,2,1.5\) in sensor units. Successive differences are \(-2,-1,-0.5\), a geometric sequence with ratio \(q=e^{-k}=1/2\). The rate is \(k=\ln2\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\), and substituting the first two points gives

\[a=4,b=1.\]

At the correct rate the profile residual vanishes. An independent numerical implementation should reproduce both the rate and these coefficients and show \(X^Tr\) near zero for every rate, including incorrect candidate rates. These checks distinguish errors in profiling from failures of the outer search.

The difference ratio gives a poor general estimator when the later differences approach the measurement noise. Its numerator and denominator share observed samples, so their errors are correlated. Profiling the original observations uses all data without creating these ratio singularities and permits weighting by known variances. A late-time outlier can nonetheless bias a nonlinear fit with only a handful of samples. Examine the four individual residuals and compare predictions at a held-out time before interpreting a narrow numerical minimum as an identified physical mechanism.

Inspect the rate profile after refitting amplitude and background

The calculation uses the four times above and adds a controlled perturbation to one late observation. It solves the two linear nuisance parameters afresh at each trial rate; a second curve evaluates the same rate with amplitude and background fixed to their original noiseless values. Comparing them makes visible how allowing nuisance parameters to adapt changes both the best rate and the apparent uncertainty. At zero perturbation both curves share \(k=\ln2\), but only the profiled curve describes the stipulated joint fit.

Open this experiment online

What precision would the four measurements support?

Let the four observations have independent, equal standard deviation \(\sigma\). A small change in rate has derivative vector \(v_i=-a t_i e^{-kt_i}\). Refitting amplitude and background can mimic any change in the span of \(s_i=e^{-kt_i}\) and the constant vector. Only the orthogonal component \(v_\perp=(I-P_X)v\) distinguishes the rate. At the baseline \(a=4,\ k=\log 2\), the unprojected derivative is \((0,-2,-2,-1.5)\). Regressing it on \(s\) and a constant gives slope \(1.98261\) and intercept \(-2.30435\). Subtract that fit and add the squared remainders to obtain

\[\|v_\perp\|^2=0.92174.\]

The local least-squares change in rate due to a noise vector is \(v_\perp^T\epsilon/\|v_\perp\|^2\). Its standard deviation is therefore \(\sigma/\|v_\perp\|\). For \(\sigma=0.10\) this is approximately \(0.104\ {\rm s}^{-1}\). Tiny tolerances in the scalar optimiser cannot supply additional information. The estimate assumes small perturbations and an adequate decay law. A visibly asymmetric profile requires inspecting the actual objective on both sides before attaching symmetric error bars.

Tutorial · distinguish a narrow numerical minimum from identified data

Check a false rate numerically

For the noiseless observations take \(q=e^{-k}=1/4\). Compute the best linear background and amplitude approximately. Verify that the sum of residuals vanishes although the fit is wrong.

Work through the solution

Here \(s=(1,0.25,0.0625,0.015625)\). Using the centred expressions gives \(a\simeq3.325,\ b\simeq1.771\). The mean prediction equals the observed mean \(2.875\) by construction, forcing the residual sum to zero. The individual residuals are not all zero because their successive differences have ratio \(1/2\) instead of \(1/4\). Orthogonality to fitted columns certifies the conditional linear solve, not the outer rate.

Measure how the profile changes if the sampling interval doubles

Suppose the same four numerical readings are obtained at \(0,2,4,6\) s. Which parameters change, and which ratio argument still works?

Work through the solution

The decay per sample remains \(q=0.5\), but now \(q=e^{-2k}\), so

\[k=(\ln2)/2=0.3466\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}.\]

Amplitude and baseline stay 4 and 1. Reporting \(q\) as a per-second rate without including time spacing would create a factor-of-two physical error even though every fitted residual vanishes.

Identify a structurally invisible rate

Take a preparation with true amplitude \(a=0\), independent equal-variance noise and an unknown baseline. Can any choice of times identify \(k\) from the exact noiseless data?

Work through the solution

For every \(k>0\) the inner fit permits \(a=0,b=\bar y\), giving zero residual for constant data. The local rate derivative \(X'c\) vanishes and the profile carries no rate information. Random noise may select a numerical minimum, but it does not turn the true no-decay signal into an identified relaxation rate. The experiment needs a preparation with measurable nonzero excitation or a different observable sensitive to the rate.

  • Golub and Pereyra · separable nonlinear least squares and variable projection
  • Course investigation · profiles and predictive uncertainty
