Inferring parameters and fields from observations · 2. Choose a regularisation scale by predicting omitted data

Regularised least squares can fit each observation partly with itself. Derive leave-one-out residuals from a single factorisation, approximate them by a trace and identify when a generalised cross-validation score misses a highly influential sensor.

Express a regularised fit as a map on measured data

Let whitened observations \(y\in\mathbb R^n\) be modelled by \(Ax\), and for \(\lambda>0\) minimise \(\|Ax-y\|^2+\lambda\|x\|^2\). The minimiser satisfies

\[(A^TA+\lambda I)x=A^Ty.\]

Predicted data are \(\hat y=H_\lambda y\), where \(H_\lambda=A(A^TA+\lambda I)^{-1}A^T\). This “hat” matrix maps the observations to their own fitted values. If \(A=U\Sigma V^T\), its nonzero eigenvalues are

\[h_j=\sigma_j^2/(\sigma_j^2+\lambda).\]

Their sum \(d(\lambda)=\operatorname{tr}H_\lambda\) is an effective degrees-of-freedom count. A weakly determined direction with small singular value can contribute a fraction of an independently fitted parameter.

The norm of the training residual decreases or stays constant as the penalty weakens. For a square full-rank model it tends to zero as the penalty tends to zero, even with noisy data. Individual residuals can change in either direction. It does not measure out-of-sample error. Instead ask how well the fitted model predicts each observation when it was left out. Repeating the full solve \(n\) times conceptually works but wastes structure. An exact deletion identity gives each left-out residual \(r_i^{(-i)}=(y_i-\hat y_i)/(1-H_{ii})\), where \(H_{ii}\) is the observation's leverage. To derive the formula, remove row \(a_i^T\), subtracting \(a_i a_i^T\) from the penalised normal matrix. Multiplying its inverse update by the original residual along \(a_i\) gives the factor \(1/(1-a_i^TG^{-1}a_i)\), with \(G=A^TA+\lambda I\). This derivation assumes the same penalty strength in the objective for the held-out and full fits.

Replace individual influences by their average only once

The leave-one-out score is the mean of \([r_i/(1-H_{ii})]^2\). Generalised cross-validation replaces the individual diagonal elements by the common mean \(d/n\), giving

\[{\rm GCV}(\lambda)=\frac{\|y-\hat y\|^2/n}{(1-d(\lambda)/n)^2}.\]

When every leverage is equal, this approximation reproduces leave-one-out exactly. Its expression is invariant to orthogonal transformations of the whitened data space because both trace and residual norm are invariant. Exact leave-one-observation-out instead depends on which physical observations are removed, which matters for a particular instrument arrangement. GCV is useful when a rotationally averaged prediction criterion is appropriate; it is not a universal interpretation of removing an actual sensor or predicting an unmeasured parameter direction.

Consider \(A=(1,1,1)^T\) fitting a scalar common signal, \(y=(1.1,0.9,1.0)\), and \(\lambda=1\). The fit is \(\hat x=3/4=0.75\), so residuals are \(0.35,0.15,0.25\). All leverages are \(1/4\). Exact prediction errors on deleting each datum are residuals divided by 0.75. For example removing 1.1 leaves \((0.9+1.0)/(2+1)=0.6333\), giving 0.4667 error, equal to \(0.35/0.75\). GCV equals exact leave-one-out here. Clear agreement in this symmetric scalar case should be a first numerical verification before interpreting a higher-dimensional score whose influences vary.

Background · What counts as an independent observation when validating?

Suppose each specimen is read twice on the same instrument, and its calibration offset changes only between specimens. Omitting one reading leaves its twin available to the fit. A low single-reading validation error then describes another reading of an already observed specimen. It can be optimistic for the next specimen, whose offset is unknown.

For an omitted group \(G\), the scalar denominator \(1-h_{ii}\) from leave-one-out becomes a small matrix. With fixed regularisation, unchanged data weights and squared loss, the group prediction residual satisfies

\[(I-H_{GG})\,r_G^{(-G)}=r_G.\]

The off-diagonal entries account for the influence that the two readings exert on each other. If removing an entire group leaves a poorly constrained direction, the solve amplifies that direction. Match the omitted group to the intended future experiment before choosing a score.

See how one sensitive observation can dominate a fit

For another scalar design \(A=(1,1,4)^T\) with \(\lambda=2\), \(G=20\) and \(H_{ii}=(0.05,0.05,0.80)\). A residual on the third sensor is multiplied by five in a true deletion while the first two are amplified only slightly. GCV uses the same factor \(1/(1-0.9/3)=1.429\) for all. If the model discrepancy is concentrated in that high-leverage reading, trace averaging can underestimate the consequence of losing it. Conversely if errors fall only on low-leverage sensors it can overstate their delete-one loss. Inspecting the leverage distribution is inexpensive compared with attributing precision to a single scalar tuning criterion.

Whitening rotates and rescales observations when errors are correlated. Leaving out one row after a dense whitening transformation is generally not the same experiment as removing one physical sensor before whitening. A time-correlated series may demand holding out an entire time block so training data cannot predict a held-out noise realisation through short-range correlation. Predictive selection should specify whether the intended prediction concerns a new repeat at known conditions, a missing detector or a new range of the physical parameters. A penalty that works well for a data prediction need not minimise reconstruction error in a weakly observable component of \(x\).

When does using the average leverage change the answer?

Four sensors have sensitivity one. Their readings are 1.10, 0.90, 1.05 and 0.95. The fifth reading equals its sensitivity plus an adjustable offset. First give all five equal sensitivity. The two scores coincide even with a nonzero offset. Now set the fifth sensitivity to 5 and its offset to 0.8. At λ = 1, compare the fifth leverage with the mean leverage. Predict the fifth reading from the other four, then use this single omitted-sensor error to check which score can reach 0.66.

The OneSimulation model refits five separate reduced data sets for every penalty. The first CSV score therefore measures actual omitted-sensor predictions. Compare it point by point with the faster identity used in the browser.

Tutorial · recover two kinds of influence from one factorisation

Compute the symmetric-case score

Using the common-signal example, evaluate GCV and verify it by explicit deletion of all three observations.

Work through the solution

The residual squared sum is

\[0.35^2+0.15^2+0.25^2=0.2075.\]

Its mean divided by \(0.75^2\) gives 0.12296. The three exact predictive residuals are \(0.4667,0.2000,0.3333\); squaring and averaging reproduces 0.12296. The unit of this score is squared whitened observation units. Taking its square root gives an rms predicted datum error, not a standard deviation of the fitted underlying parameter.

Recover effective dimension from singular values

A whitened model has singular values \(4,1,0.1\) and six readings. For \(\lambda=1\), calculate \(d\) and the GCV multiplier applied to mean square residual.

Work through the solution

The gains are

\[16/17=0.9412,\ 1/2,\ 0.01/1.01=0.00990.\]

Their sum is 1.4511 and \(1/[1-d/6]^2\simeq1.740\). The weak singular direction barely enters the prediction even though the algebraic rank is three. With vanishing penalty all three nonzero gains tend to one; with very large penalty they approach zero. This continuous change is why a count of nonzero columns cannot replace the trace in the score.

Diagnose misleading use of the diagonal basis

For \(A=I\) of size \(n\) and the same ridge penalty, compute GCV for every positive \(\lambda\). Can these data identify a preferred penalty from that score?

Work through the solution

Here \(H=I/(1+\lambda)\),

\[r=\lambda y/(1+\lambda),\]
\[1-d/n=\lambda/(1+\lambda).\]

Cancellation gives \({\rm GCV}=\|y\|^2/n\), independent of \(\lambda\). Leaving one coordinate out leaves no data at all about that independent parameter, so the prediction by the penalised system for that coordinate is always zero. A numerical minimum created by roundoff does not select a meaningful smoothing strength. Structural correlations, replication, a noise model or external validation are needed to decide how much shrinkage helps the intended task.

  • Course investigation · regularisation with singular data

Inferring parameters and fields from observations · 3. Recovering sparse coefficients without hiding amplitude bias

A sensor records a linear mixture of a few active components. Derive the soft-threshold map, use it in an iterative reconstruction and decide when correlated response columns make an apparently sharp sparse answer ambiguous.

Derive a nonsmooth optimum before choosing a solver

Let a whitened linear model be \(y=Ax+\eta\) and consider minimising \(F(x)=\|Ax-y\|^2/2+\lambda\sum_j|x_j|\). The parameter \(\lambda\ge0\) balances fit with sparsity in this chosen coordinate system. Rescaling one column of \(A\) while leaving its penalty unchanged changes how expensive the corresponding physical component is; normalise columns or use documented coefficient-specific weights before comparing support selection across different quantities. Sparsity of a signal in one basis is a modelling assumption, not evidence generated by the penalty itself.

For an orthonormal design \(A^TA=I\), expanding the square shows that minimisation separates into scalar terms \((x_j-z_j)^2/2+\lambda|x_j|\), with \(z=A^Ty\). For a positive solution the derivative is \(x-z+\lambda=0\); for a negative one it is \(x-z-\lambda=0\). At zero the absolute-value subgradient is the interval \([-1,1]\), so zero is optimal when \(|z|\le\lambda\). These cases give

\[S_\lambda(z)=\operatorname{sign}(z)\max(|z|-\lambda,0).\]

Unlike simply removing small coefficients, soft thresholding also shrinks every retained amplitude by \(\lambda\). This predictable bias can matter even if the selected support is physically correct.

For \(z=(2.4,-0.7,0.18)\) and \(\lambda=0.5\), the solution is \((1.9,-0.2,0)\). The residual correlations \(A^T(y-Ax)\) must equal \(\lambda\) times the sign on active coordinates and stay in \([-\lambda,\lambda]\) on inactive coordinates. Here they are \((0.5,-0.5,0.18)\). These conditions independently certify optimality for the convex objective, without proving the true sample actually contains only two active mechanisms.

Majorise the smooth loss and solve its local model exactly

For a general \(A\), write \(f(x)=\|Ax-y\|^2/2\) and \(\nabla f=A^T(Ax-y)\). Taylor expansion is exact with second-order term \(\|A(z-x)\|^2/2\le L\|z-x\|^2/2\), \(L=\|A\|_2^2\). Replace the true quadratic term by this upper bound, keep the penalty exact and minimise over \(z\). Completing the square produces

\[x^{k+1}=S_{\lambda/L}\left(x^k-\frac1L A^T(Ax^k-y)\right)\]

componentwise. This proximal-gradient or ISTA step decreases the objective for this step bound. An under-estimated \(L\) can break the majorisation. A backtracking check evaluates the true smooth loss at the candidate instead of requiring an exact power-method estimate of the largest singular value. Acceleration can reduce the number of iterations, while a numerical termination test based on the subgradient or objective gap remains necessary.

Take \(A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right), y=(1,0)^T,\lambda=0.2\). Its spectral \(L=(3+\sqrt5)/2=2.618\). Starting at zero, \(A^Ty=(1,1)\), so the first iterate is

\[(0.8/L,0.8/L)\simeq(0.3056,0.3056).\]

Both components initially enter equally even though the first column alone fits the pattern exactly. The second measurement penalises a nonzero second coefficient. At the optimum \((0.8,0)\), residual correlations are \((0.2,0.2)\), so the inactive second coefficient sits exactly on its subgradient boundary. A small data perturbation can change its selected status, despite a well-defined convex optimum for this full-rank matrix. Stability of objective value and stability of support are different questions.

Interpret support and debias only after validating it

If two columns of \(A\) are identical, two nonnegative amplitudes with the same sum give the same predictions and the same \(\ell^1\) penalty. A solver may split an identifiable total between the columns or report one arbitrary active component. Lowering the residual tolerance will not resolve this ambiguity. Acquire a measurement where the columns respond differently, merge them if only the total is scientifically meaningful or report a range of admissible allocations. Near-collinear columns give an approximate version of this effect that can be revealed by perturbing the data within their measured noise or holding out a sensor condition.

A common second step is to refit by ordinary or weighted least squares on the selected support to remove shrinkage bias. For the orthonormal example it would restore the retained amplitudes to \(2.4,-0.7\). This is a conditional estimate after choosing that support; it does not undo selection effects and can amplify noise strongly for a nearly singular active submatrix. Test the selected support and refitted prediction on held-out observations. An uncertainty interval that assumes the support known in advance can be too optimistic when small perturbations switch the active set.

Can support and amplitudes both be correct?

The six orthogonal channels have three true nonzero coefficients. Set the perturbation multiplier to one and start with zero threshold. Increase the threshold just far enough to remove the three noise-only channels. Does the weak third signal survive? Now set the perturbation to zero while keeping that threshold fixed. Compare the third estimate with its true value to measure the shrinkage introduced by the penalty itself.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · verify an inverse solution using subgradients

Find the penalty where everything switches off

For a general \(A,y\), derive the smallest \(\lambda\) for which \(x=0\) is optimal. Evaluate for the small matrix above.

Work through the solution

At zero, stationarity requires \(A^Ty\in\lambda[-1,1]^p\). Thus \(\lambda_{\max}=\|A^Ty\|_\infty\), which equals 1 in the example. At any larger penalty no component is supported by its residual correlation relative to the threshold. At exact equality an inactive coordinate can lie on the boundary. The criterion concerns the scale of whitened data and normalised design and changes when units or weights change.

Compare soft thresholding with hard component deletion

For a scalar reading 1.2 and \(\lambda=0.5\), compare objectives at the soft estimate, raw reading and zero. Which is lowest and what does it show?

Work through the solution

The soft estimate is 0.7, with objective \(0.5(0.5)^2+0.5(0.7)=0.475\). At 1.2 the residual vanishes but penalty yields 0.600; at zero the loss is 0.720. The shrinkage is a deliberate consequence of the stated objective rather than an error of an iterative algorithm. If amplitude preservation is important, a separate support-conditioned refit answers a different optimisation question.

Prove nonidentifiability with two equal columns

Take \(A=(1,1)\), \(y=3\) and \(\lambda=1\). Characterise an entire family of optimal nonnegative solutions and the unique total that data and penalty identify.

Work through the solution

On nonnegative inputs the objective depends only on \(s=x_1+x_2\) as \((s-3)^2/2+s\). Its minimum is at \(s=2\). The triangle inequality shows no signed decomposition of that same sum can lower its penalty. Every pair \((q,2-q)\) for \(0\le q\le2\) is therefore globally optimal. An algorithm returning \((2,0)\) has not established that the first physical species is present and the second absent. Only their aggregate is determined without a distinguishing observation or another justified prior.

Further reading

  • Beck and Teboulle · iterative shrinkage-thresholding for linear inverse problems
  • Course investigation · inverse problems with nonsmooth constraints

Inferring parameters and fields from observations · 4. What each new sensor reading changes in a Gaussian inverse problem

Start with an uncertain vector, condition on one noisy linear observation, and reconstruct the posterior mean and covariance without repeating a batch inversion. Check shared calibration errors before treating repeated measurements as independent evidence.

Condition a joint Gaussian by eliminating a correlated component

Let \(x\) have a Gaussian prior with mean \(m\) and positive definite covariance \(P\). A scalar instrument returns \(y=h^Tx+\epsilon\), with zero-mean Gaussian noise independent of \(x\) of variance \(r>0\). The predicted observation mean is \(h^Tm\); the innovation \(d=y-h^Tm\) has variance \(S=h^TPh+r\), and cross covariance of \(x\) with this innovation is \(Ph\). Seek a corrected error \((x-m)-Kd\) uncorrelated with \(d\). Its covariance with \(d\) vanishes when \(K=Ph/S\). Because the joint distribution is Gaussian, uncorrelated then implies independent, so conditioning on the observed \(d\) gives

\[m^+=m+K d,\]
\[P^+=P-\frac{Phh^TP}{S}.\]

The covariance correction is rank one and positive semidefinite when subtracted. It depends on geometry and declared noise, not the particular measurement value. The new mean can move strongly when a large innovation occurs even if the predicted covariance reduction was modest. A very unlikely innovation is reason to check the physical model and reported uncertainty before proceeding as though the calibration were known. Recomputing an ad hoc smaller measurement variance after seeing the data would change the probabilistic assumptions.

An equivalent route minimises \((x-m)^TP^{-1}(x-m)+(y-h^Tx)^2/r\), yielding posterior precision \(P^{-1}+hh^T/r\). Multiplying by the proposed covariance above verifies its inverse directly, a one-row Woodbury identity. For several independent readings, applying rank-one updates sequentially produces the same posterior as one batch solve in exact arithmetic. If the instrument errors are correlated, either whiten the full observation model or explicitly include shared error components in the state before applying independent updates. The matrix identity is exact only for the covariance actually specified in the statistical model.

A correlated prior transfers information to an unmeasured coordinate

Set \(m=(0,0)^T\), \(P=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&1\\1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\). Measure only the first coordinate with \(h=(1,0)^T,\ r=1,\ y=3\). The innovation variance is 5 and the gain is \((0.8,0.2)^T\). Hence

\[m^+=(2.4,0.6),\]
\[P^+=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0.8&0.2\\0.2&0.8\end{smallmatrix}\right).\]

The second coordinate moved even though the instrument does not directly sense it. That inference uses prior correlation. If prior off-diagonal covariance were zero, its posterior mean and variance would remain unchanged. A conclusion about an unobserved material parameter should therefore report whether sensitivity comes from data or from that imposed correlation structure.

The innovation itself is \(3/\sqrt5=1.34\) predicted standard deviations, plausible under the original model. Conditioned residual at the same reading becomes \(3-2.4=0.6\), much smaller because that observation helped fit the estimate. A model check should use the pre-update innovation or an independent held-out observation. In a sequence of independent conditionally generated data, correctly normalised innovations provide a useful diagnostic for error scale and neglected correlation. An extremely small training residual after every assimilation can simply reflect a model that overweights its own observations.

Maintain a valid covariance over many updates

In exact arithmetic \(P^+=P-Kh^TP\). In finite precision subtracting two nearly equal matrices can erode symmetry or produce a small negative eigenvalue in a direction known very precisely. The equivalent Joseph form \(P^+=(I-Kh^T)P(I-Kh^T)^T+KrK^T\) expresses the result as a sum of positive semidefinite pieces. It also derives directly from the prior state error and independent measurement noise entering the update. A square-root covariance or information factor can improve numerical robustness over repeated strongly informative observations. Simply setting negative diagonal entries to zero after a bad update may still leave an indefinite correlated matrix.

If the state evolves between observations with \(x_{\rm next}=Fx+w\), independent process noise covariance \(Q\) gives predicted mean \(Fm^+\) and covariance \(FP^+F^T+Q\). Choosing \(Q=0\) asserts exact dynamics; it is not a harmless way to remove an implementation parameter. Repeated observations can then drive covariance toward zero in a falsely rigid physical model. Conversely arbitrarily increasing process noise to make any data appear plausible sacrifices the predictive test. Estimate or validate process variability independently when deciding whether a sequential inverse problem represents one stationary parameter or an evolving hidden state.

One hundred readings with one shared calibration

Two parameters have unit prior variance and adjustable correlation. Observe the first parameter repeatedly. Each reading has independent Gaussian noise of standard deviation 0.4 plus the same Gaussian calibration offset, drawn independently of the parameters and the individual noise. First set the calibration uncertainty to zero. Restore it and compare the limiting widths. Finally set the prior correlation to zero and decide which reading could possibly update the second parameter.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · separate new information from repeated calibration

Assimilate an independent second measurement

Continue from the two-coordinate example and measure the second coordinate with \(h=(0,1)^T,\ r=0.2,\ y=1.1\). Compute the updated mean and covariance.

Work through the solution

The predicted reading is 0.6, innovation 0.5 and \(S=0.8+0.2=1\). The gain is \((0.2,0.8)^T\), giving mean \((2.5,1.0)\). Subtract \(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0.04&0.16\\0.16&0.64\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) from the preceding covariance, giving \(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0.76&0.04\\0.04&0.16\end{smallmatrix}\right)\). Its determinant is 0.12 and both diagonal entries are positive, consistent with a positive definite remaining uncertainty.

Quantify a shared calibration floor

A scalar prior has variance 4. Two or more readings each have independent noise variance 1 and share the same unknown additive calibration bias of variance 0.25. Find posterior variance of the scalar from \(n\) readings and its limit.

Work through the solution

The sample mean measures the scalar plus shared bias with variance \(0.25+1/n\); independent deviations of readings from their mean contain no information about the scalar in this Gaussian model. Thus posterior variance is \([1/4+1/(0.25+1/n)]^{-1}\), tending to \(1/4.25=0.2353\). Incorrectly assigning all errors independent would make the posterior variance vanish. An independent reference sensitive to the calibration bias can lower this floor.

Choose a sensor for a requested derived quantity

Two candidate sensors of the same noise cost are available. Derive the covariance reduction in \(q^Tx\) and evaluate for \(q=(0,1)\), the original \(P\), \(r=1\) and direct measurements of either coordinate.

Work through the solution

The variance reduction is \((q^TPh)^2/(h^TPh+r)\). Measuring coordinate 1 lowers the desired variance by \(1/5=0.2\); measuring coordinate 2 lowers it by \(1/2=0.5\). The larger variance of coordinate 1 could make it an attractive sensor target under a different design criterion, yet the specified prediction favours direct measurement of coordinate 2. State the scientific quantity whose uncertainty the design should reduce.

Inferring parameters and fields from observations · 5. What can unfinished endurance tests reveal about lifetime?

When a test stops, a surviving bearing has supplied information. Derive the likelihood from first principles and examine how changing the observation deadline moves the rate inferred from a small batch.

Count failures and add the time spent on test

Assume identical bearings fail independently at a constant rate \(\lambda\) per hour. Over a short interval \(dt\), the conditional probability of failure for an operating bearing is \(\lambda\,dt+o(dt)\). This gives the differential equation \(S'(t)=-\lambda S(t)\) with \(S(0)=1\) for the fraction surviving beyond \(t\). Its solution is \(S(t)=e^{-\lambda t}\) and the failure-time density is

\[f(t)=\lambda e^{-\lambda t}.\]

Choose a fixed administrative deadline \(c\) in advance. Each observed duration is the earlier of the failure time and \(c\). Write \(\delta_i=1\) for a recorded failure, zero for an unfinished run. A failure contributes its density. An unfinished run contributes the probability of lasting at least its recorded duration. Multiplying these quantities over the bearings gives

\[L(\lambda)=\prod_i f(t_i)^{\delta_i}S(t_i)^{1-\delta_i} =\lambda^d e^{-\lambda T},\]
\[d=\sum_i\delta_i,\]
\[T=\sum_i t_i.\]

For positive \(d\), differentiating \(d\log\lambda-\lambda T\) gives a score \(d/\lambda-T\). It vanishes at \(\hat\lambda=d/T\). The curvature \(-d/\lambda^2\) is negative. With zero failures the likelihood falls immediately to the right of zero, making zero the boundary maximum. A survivor contributes no failure to \(d\) and still adds positive exposure to \(T\). Removal triggered by a bearing's imminent failure would require a model for that mechanism. A predeclared deadline avoids this selection issue.

Audit a batch stopped after seven hours

Suppose the simulated complete lifetimes are 2, 4, 6, 9 and 14 hours. At seven hours the actual records are three failures and two unfinished tests. Their total exposure is \(2+4+6+7+7=26\) bearing-hours. The rate estimate is \(3/26=0.11538\) per hour and the fitted mean lifetime is \(26/3=8.667\) hours. Discarding the two survivors leaves 12 bearing-hours and inflates the apparent rate to \(3/12=0.25\) per hour.

For a candidate rate of 0.20 per hour the log likelihood relative to the full-data maximum is

\[3\log(0.20/(3/26))-26(0.20-3/26)\approx-0.550.\]

This is a relative likelihood near 0.58. The absolute density changes when time units change, while this likelihood ratio is invariant. In the notebook change the deadline to five hours. Each failure that now occurs after the deadline becomes an unfinished observation. Inspect the time lines before interpreting the new estimate.

Exercise · change the stopping time

What does a five-hour deadline yield for the same five bearings? Can extending the deadline without any additional failure increase the estimated rate?

Work through the solution

The durations are 2, 4, 5, 5 and 5 hours with two observed failures. The new estimate is \(2/21=0.09524\) per hour. While additional operating time accumulates with the same failure count, the ratio \(d/T\) falls for positive \(d\). The estimate can jump upward at an actual failure event. A generally increasing or decreasing trend with a moving deadline is therefore not guaranteed.

Exercise · infer an upper rate after zero failures

Five fresh bearings all survive ten hours. Find the rate for which observing no failures would have probability 0.05 under the model.

Work through the solution

All five must survive ten hours. Independence gives probability \(e^{-50\lambda}\). Setting this equal to 0.05 gives \(\lambda=-\log(0.05)/50\approx0.05991\) per hour. Inverting this zero-failure event gives a one-sided 95% upper confidence bound for the rate in this experiment. The likelihood maximum itself remains at zero.

Inferring parameters and fields from observations · 6. How an integral instrument becomes a matrix

Construct two discrete models of the same measurement, diagnose noise with a Picard plot and test a regularisation parameter against a deliberately imperfect instrument model.

Derive the matrix twice from the same kernel

Suppose an instrument measures \(b(s)=\int_0^1 K(s,t)x(t)\,dt+e(s)\). With square-integrable kernel this forward operator is compact on \(L^2\). Small singular values suppress some source directions. For a midpoint rule with source nodes \(t_j\) and weights \(w_j\), collocation at receiver nodes \(s_i\) gives \(b_i\simeq\sum_j K(s_i,t_j)w_j x(t_j)\). To compare spectra over nonuniform meshes, use the variables \(\sqrt{w_j}x(t_j)\) and data \(\sqrt{v_i}b(s_i)\). The Euclidean matrix is \(A_{ij}=\sqrt{v_i}K(s_i,t_j)\sqrt{w_j}\). Noise whitening is an additional operation using the measurement covariance.

Projection instead represents \(x=\sum_j c_j\phi_j\) and tests against \(\psi_i\). For orthonormal bases the matrix and data are

\[G_{ij}=\int_0^1\int_0^1\psi_i(s)K(s,t)\phi_j(t)\,dt\,ds,\]
\[d_i=\int_0^1\psi_i(s)b(s)\,ds.\]

For cell indicators normalised by the square root of cell length this double integral averages the response over both the detector and the source footprint. For nonorthonormal bases use mass matrices to measure physical norms. Omitting these metrics changes the meaning of singular values as the mesh changes.

Take \(K=1+st\), two equal cells and coincident midpoint receivers. The weighted midpoint and cell-average projection agree because the kernel is affine in each argument. The two matrices equal one half times the matrix with rows \((17/16,19/16),(19/16,25/16)\). Their determinant is \(1/16\). Changing to \(K=1+s^2t^2\) gives midpoint squares \(1/16,9/16\) while their cell averages are \(1/12,7/12\). The resulting spectra differ even with exact arithmetic. Refining both grids and inspecting the spectrum that stays above the data-noise floor distinguishes discretisation from information.

Read data and singular values together

For \(A=U\Sigma V^T\), the noise-free continuum solution has finite energy if \(\sum |u_i^Tb_{\rm exact}/\sigma_i|^2<\infty\), with no inaccessible data component. With independent whitened noise of similar variance in each left singular direction, \(|u_i^Tb|\) eventually follows a roughly flat noise floor. A Picard diagram plots \(\sigma_i\), \(|u_i^Tb|\) and their quotient against index. For an exact quotient proportional to \(i^{-1}\) the squared sum converges. If the noise coefficients have typical size \(\delta\) and \(\sigma_i\sim i^{-2}\), their quotients typically grow like \(\delta i^2\). The index where the data cease to decay depends on the observation and its noise.

Derive what an L-curve actually plots

For identity Tikhonov and strength \(\mu\), write \(b_i=u_i^Tb\) and \(f_i=\sigma_i^2/(\sigma_i^2+\mu^2)\). The residual and solution coordinates of the L-curve are

\[R^2=\sum_i(1-f_i)^2 b_i^2+\|b_\perp\|^2,\]
\[S^2=\sum_i(f_i b_i/\sigma_i)^2 .\]

Mark \((\log R,\log S)\) against \(\log\mu\). A bend can reveal the transition from fitting signal to amplifying noise. With \(\eta=\log\mu\) its curvature magnitude is \(|X'Y''-Y'X''|/(X'^2+Y'^2)^{3/2}\) for \(X=\log R,Y=\log S\). Sparse sampling or multiple spectral clusters can give competing curvature peaks. If \(b\) has energy in only one singular direction, the curve reflects the scale of that mode even with zero noise. A corner by itself then provides no data-driven noise estimate. Compare with a discrepancy level justified by instrument noise, or held-out predictive error and GCV.

For a reproducible ambiguous corner choose singular values \((1,s)\) and data coefficients \((1,\sqrt{s})\), with \(s=10^{-3}\). Replacing \(\mu\) by \(s/\mu\) swaps each filter with the complement of the other. Substitution gives \(R(s/\mu)=\sqrt{s}\,S(\mu)\) and \(S(s/\mu)=R(\mu)/\sqrt{s}\). In log coordinates this transformation is a reflection and preserves curvature magnitude. The two outward bends near \(\mu=s\) and \(\mu=1\) therefore have equal curvature. These choices differ by roughly a thousand in regularisation strength. If the second measured coefficient is reliable, damping it away costs information. If that coefficient is mostly noise, keeping it produces a state component amplified by \(1/s\). The shape of this curve cannot decide which measurement assumption applies.

OneSimulation experiment · Reconstruct data from an independently discretised instrument

Vary the true blur width or noise seed. Decide which feature persists across plausible instruments.

Download the executable notebook · Python source

Adjust the experiment in the browser. Start with matching blur widths and zero noise. Increase the noise, then vary \(\mu\) to trade oscillatory reconstruction against loss of the small-scale signal. Finally change only the true width. A small data residual can now coexist with a biased reconstruction. Every adjustment regenerates the data on 1024 source cells and solves the 32-variable regularised normal system by Cholesky factorisation. The browser keeps one reproducible noise pattern so a slider movement isolates a parameter change. The downloadable OneSimulation model below makes the same distinction between data and inversion grids and adds spectral diagnostics; its random generator produces a different noise sample.

Open the live experiment

"""Observe model error in a periodic deblurring experiment on different grids."""
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
from onesim.numerics.linear.matrices.dense_linear_algebra import solve_dense

def instrument(receivers, sources, width):
    delta = (receivers[:, None] - sources[None, :] + 0.5) % 1 - 0.5
    kernel = np.exp(-(delta/width)**2/2)
    return kernel / kernel.sum(axis=1)[:, None]

def signal(x):
    return np.cos(2*np.pi*x) + 0.4*np.sin(10*np.pi*x)

fine = (np.arange(1024)+0.5)/1024
coarse = (np.arange(32)+0.5)/32
design = instrument(coarse, coarse, 0.045)
rng = np.random.default_rng(17)
noise = rng.normal(0, 0.002, coarse.size)
strengths = np.logspace(-4, -0.4, 32)
fig, (picard, resolution, ax) = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(11.2, 4), layout="constrained")
left, singular, _ = np.linalg.svd(design, full_matrices=False)
indices = np.arange(1, len(singular)+1)
picard.semilogy(indices, singular, color="black", label="Singular values")
for trial in [0.01, 0.1]:
    gain = singular**2 / (singular**2 + trial**2)
    resolution.plot(indices, gain, label=f"μ = {trial:g}")
resolution.set(xlabel="Mode", ylabel="Resolution gain")
resolution.legend()
for width in [0.045, 0.065]:
    data = instrument(coarse, fine, width) @ signal(fine) + noise
    picard.semilogy(indices, abs(left.T @ data), ".", label=f"Data, width {width}")
    errors = []
    for mu in strengths:
        state = solve_dense(design.T @ design + mu**2*np.eye(coarse.size), design.T @ data)
        errors.append(np.linalg.norm(state-signal(coarse))/np.sqrt(coarse.size))
    print(f"True width {width:.3f}: minimum grid RMS error {min(errors):.5f}")
    ax.loglog(strengths, errors, label=f"True instrument width {width}")
ax.set(xlabel="Tikhonov parameter", ylabel="Reconstruction RMS error")
ax.legend(fontsize=8)
picard.set(xlabel="Mode", ylabel="Magnitude")
picard.legend(fontsize=8)
fig.savefig("numerics-inverse-blur.png", dpi=170)

Output from the reference run

True width 0.045: minimum grid RMS error 0.01013
True width 0.065: minimum grid RMS error 0.18949
Why separate the generation and inversion grids?

Matrices on the same grid share quadrature and representation errors. Synthesising \(b=Ax\) and inverting with exactly that \(A\) omits those errors from the test. A finer source grid and slightly perturbed blur width create two separately controllable discrepancies. The reconstruction notebook shows how the best achievable error changes when only the assumed width is wrong.

Tutorial on weighted spectra and resolution

Calculate the two singular values of the midpoint example

Work through the solution

The matrix is symmetric positive definite with trace \(21/16\) and determinant \(1/16\). Its eigenvalues are \((21\pm\sqrt{377})/32\), also its singular values. The smaller mode is much more sensitive to perturbations in the measurements.

Distinguish variance reduction from regained resolution

Work through the solution

In singular direction \(i\) the mean reconstruction of a true coefficient \(x_i\) is \(f_ix_i\) and independent data noise of variance \(\delta^2\) contributes variance \(\delta^2 f_i^2/\sigma_i^2\). Larger \(\mu\) lowers both gain and variance. A small repeat-to-repeat spread can coexist with a strongly suppressed feature.

Hansen · discrete inverse problems and teaching materials

Inferring parameters and fields from observations · 7. Early stopping and penalties that leave part of a field free

Derive two matrix-free least-squares iterations, find where noise first enters their solution and separate the measured and penalised directions of a nonstandard Tikhonov problem.

Obtain a full iteration without forming the normal matrix

Let \(r=b-Ax,\ s=A^Tr\). Applying conjugate gradients to \(A^TAx=A^Tb\) starting at \(x=0\) works on the range of \(A^T\), where the operator is positive definite even if \(A\) has a nullspace. Initialise \(p=s\) and \(\gamma=s^Ts\). Along \(p\) the residual minimisation gives \(\alpha=(Ap)^Tr/\|Ap\|^2\). Conjugacy of earlier directions implies \(p^Ts=s^Ts\). Thus one CGLS iteration is

\[q=Ap,\]
\[\alpha=\gamma/(q^Tq),\quad x\gets x+\alpha p,\quad r\gets r-\alpha q,\quad s\gets A^Tr,\]
\[\gamma_{\rm new}=s^Ts,\quad p\gets s+(\gamma_{\rm new}/\gamma)p .\]

Replace \(\gamma\) by \(\gamma_{\rm new}\) and continue. A vanishing gradient means stationarity. Handle a zero denominator before division and recompute true residuals when finite precision matters. The recurrence needs only forward and transpose products. Exact-arithmetic search spaces are nested, so the training residual falls monotonically. The distance to the unknown state can increase.

Solve a sequence of projected rectangular problems

Normalise \(u_1=b/\beta_1\), \(\beta_1=\|b\|\), and \(v_1=A^Tu_1/\alpha_1\), \(\alpha_1=\|A^Tu_1\|\). Alternate orthogonal projections:

\[\beta_{j+1}u_{j+1}=Av_j-\alpha_j u_j,\]
\[\alpha_{j+1}v_{j+1}=A^Tu_{j+1}-\beta_{j+1}v_j .\]

These are the Golub–Kahan recurrences, with signs fixed by positive norms. In exact arithmetic induction using transposition shows both sets are orthonormal until breakdown. Collect the coefficients in a lower bidiagonal \((k+1)\)-by-\(k\) matrix \(B_k\). Since \(AV_k=U_{k+1}B_k\), minimise \(\|\beta_1e_1-B_k y\|\) and put \(x_k=V_ky\). LSQR applies plane rotations as each bidiagonal row is appended. Its exact-arithmetic iterates match CGLS started at zero. Finite precision, explicit residual checks and stopping rules affect their returned solutions.

Take \(A=\operatorname{diag}(1,0.01)\), true state \((1,0)\), measured \(b=(1,0.01)\). The first CGLS step uses \(p=(1,10^{-4})\), with \(\alpha\) essentially one, leaving the error about \(10^{-4}\). The second step can drive both residuals to zero by returning \((1,1)\), whose state error is one. A discrepancy threshold near the known noise norm \(0.01\) can stop before fitting the weak direction.

Diagonalise a matrix pair in the right metric

Consider \(\|Ax-b\|^2+\mu^2\|Lx\|^2\). Uniqueness requires \(\ker A\cap\ker L=\{0\}\). Then \(M=A^TA+L^TL\) is positive definite. Factor \(M=R^TR\). The matrices \(\widetilde A=AR^{-1}\), \(\widetilde L=LR^{-1}\) satisfy \(\widetilde A^T\widetilde A+\widetilde L^T\widetilde L=I\). Orthogonally diagonalise the first Gram matrix. In that same basis the second is diagonal with complementary nonnegative entries \(c_i^2,s_i^2\). Thus the associated right directions \(x_i=R^{-1}v_i\) satisfy \(\|Ax_i\|=c_i,\ \|Lx_i\|=s_i\) and \(c_i^2+s_i^2=1\). Their images separately are pairwise orthogonal. This construction derives the GSVD geometry. A production algorithm avoids explicitly forming ill-conditioned Gram matrices.

For \(c_i>0\) put \(u_i=Ax_i/c_i\). With \(x=\sum\xi_i x_i\), the component objective is \((c_i\xi_i-u_i^Tb)^2+\mu^2s_i^2\xi_i^2\). Minimisation gives

\[\xi_i=\frac{c_i}{c_i^2+\mu^2s_i^2}\,u_i^Tb,\qquad\]

f_i=\frac{c_i^2}{c_i^2+\mu^2s_i^2}.

An unpenalised but measured mode has \(s_i=0\) and filter one. A measured-data null direction with \(c_i=0\) has coefficient zero at the minimum. With a nonzero reference \(x_{\rm ref}\), apply the same derivation to its correction and the data \(b-Ax_{\rm ref}\).

What if both operators miss the field average?

A gradient penalty and a differential measurement can have the same constant nullspace. Adding any constant changes neither term of the objective. An extra measurement or physically specified mean is needed to fix the offset. An arbitrary minimum-norm convention changes the interpretation of the result.

Tutorial on filtered and unfiltered directions

Interpret a first-difference penalty in a two-cell experiment

Work through the solution

With \(A=I,\ L=(1,-1)\), normalised sum and difference are Euclidean eigenvectors. Their fitted gains are \(1\) and \(1/(1+2\mu^2)\). Increasing \(\mu\) enforces nearly equal cells while retaining their experimentally measured mean.

Choose a fair comparison between LSQR and CGLS

Work through the solution

Start both at zero, use identical operators and noisy data, count one forward and one transpose product per iteration, and plot reconstruction error and independent prediction error alongside the training residual. Equal iteration counts isolate finite-precision effects. Equal stopping rules test what an investigator actually reports.

Which field can be recovered from boundary data?

Relate an observation to its spatial sensitivity. Use reciprocity, smoothing with distance and changes of electrode configuration to identify recoverable features.

  3. Capacitance matrices, floating nodes and controlled approximation bounds
  4. Reconstructing a source from noisy voltage measurements
  5. Which part of a sample changes a four-electrode impedance measurement?

Green functions, the Poisson kernel and what a small electrode can influence

Electrostatic field theory and measurement

A Green function is the response of an entire boundary-value problem to a unit source. Once that response is known, a continuous charge distribution and a patterned boundary voltage can both be treated by integration. The two integrations require different kernels.

Define a point response with its boundary conditions

Let \(\Omega\) be a vacuum domain with boundary on which the voltage is given. Use the convention \(-\nabla_{\mathbf y}^2G(\mathbf x,\mathbf y)=\delta^3(\mathbf y-\mathbf x)\) and \(G=0\) on the boundary as a function of \(\mathbf y\). In free space \(G=1/(4\pi|\mathbf x-\mathbf y|)\) if decay is required at infinity. This definition places no permittivity in \(G\); physical charge enters as \(\rho/\epsilon_0\). A different frequent convention puts \(4\pi\) in the defining equation, changing all subsequent prefactors. Comparing the near-source flux to unity is a straightforward way to fix which convention is being used.

Begin with two differentiable functions \(u,v\). The difference \(\nabla\cdot(u\nabla v-v\nabla u)=u\nabla^2v-v\nabla^2u\) has no mixed-gradient term. Integrate this identity with \(u=V(\mathbf y)\),

\[v=G(\mathbf x,\mathbf y).\]

The source equation for \(G\) samples \(V\) at \(\mathbf x\), while Poisson's equation for \(V\) supplies a volume density. With the outward normal taken from \(\Omega\),

\[V(\mathbf x)=\int_\Omega G(\mathbf x,\mathbf y)\frac{\rho(\mathbf y)}{\epsilon_0}\,d^3y-\oint_{\partial\Omega}V(\mathbf y)\partial_{n_{\mathbf y}}G(\mathbf x,\mathbf y)\,dA_{\mathbf y}.\]

The \(G\partial_n V\) boundary term vanishes because \(G=0\) there. If the singularity causes concern, exclude a small sphere about \(\mathbf x\), apply the ordinary identity, and shrink the sphere. The inward normal on the excised sphere produces the same \(V(\mathbf x)\) term. The representation separates charges in the volume from voltages maintained by electrode sources; it does not combine them into one fictitious material distribution.

Solve the half-space and differentiate with respect to source position

Consider \(\mathbf x=(\mathbf X,h)\) with \(h>0\) above the plane \(z=0\). For an interior source \(\mathbf y=(\mathbf Y,z)\), denote its reflection by \(\mathbf y^*=(\mathbf Y,-z)\). The grounded half-space Green function is

\[G=\frac1{4\pi}\left[\frac1{\sqrt{|\mathbf X-\mathbf Y|^2+(h-z)^2}}-\frac1{\sqrt{|\mathbf X-\mathbf Y|^2+(h+z)^2}}\right].\]

It vanishes as the source coordinate approaches the plane. The outward domain normal there is \(-\hat{\mathbf z}\); therefore the coefficient of a prescribed boundary voltage is

\[P_h=-\partial_nG=\partial_zG|_{z=0}.\]

Differentiating the two terms gives equal positive contributions:

\[P_h(\mathbf X-\mathbf Y)=\frac{h}{2\pi[|\mathbf X-\mathbf Y|^2+h^2]^{3/2}} .\]

Its units are inverse area, while \(G\) has inverse length in this three-dimensional convention. For no interior charge \(V(\mathbf X,h)=\int P_h(\mathbf X-\mathbf Y)f(\mathbf Y)\,d^2Y\). The kernel is positive. Integrate over the whole plane using \(2\pi s\,ds\); the result is one. Hence a constant boundary voltage is reproduced, and an arbitrary voltage bounded between two numbers produces values between the same bounds inside. The integral solves the bounded half-space problem with the far-field condition appropriate to the data. Demanding decay for a nonzero constant boundary value would be inconsistent.

A detector at height \(h\) takes a weighted average over a neighbourhood of width comparable with \(h\). The kernel has algebraic tails, so its support is not a sharp circular footprint. The contribution from radii smaller than \(b\) is \(1-h/\sqrt{b^2+h^2}\). Half the total weight lies within \(b=\sqrt3h\). Ninety per cent requires

\[b=\sqrt{99}h\simeq9.95h.\]

These quite different radii show why a full-width-at-half-maximum measurement of the kernel cannot by itself define the region that contains most of its influence.

A finite driven electrode and a reciprocity experiment

Fix a circular disk of radius \(b\) in the plane at voltage \(V_d\) while grounding the rest of the plane, with ideal thin insulating seams. Directly over its centre,

\[V(0,h)=V_d\left(1-\frac{h}{\sqrt{h^2+b^2}}\right),\]
\[E_z(0,h)=V_d\frac{b^2}{(h^2+b^2)^{3/2}} .\]

At \(h\to0^+\) the electrode voltage is recovered at its centre. For \(h\gg b\) the voltage scales as \(V_d b^2/(2h^2)\). A small electrode surrounded by ground does not behave at long distance as an isolated charged electrode with a \(1/h\) potential. Its surrounding return charge makes a combined disturbance whose far field has a different order. At the sharp ideal edge the boundary voltage jumps, and local fields there require an actual seam geometry if one wants a bounded peak or a breakdown prediction.

For \(b=5.0\) mm, \(V_d=4.0\) V and \(h=5.0\) mm the axial voltage is 1.17 V and field is \(283\) V/m. A separate experiment grounds every electrode and places a point charge \(q\) at that observation point. Green reciprocity relates the net charge on the disk to the previous weighting potential \(w=V/V_d\): \(Q_d=-q\,w\). With \(q=20\) pC the disk collects \(-5.86\) pC. Direct integration of the image-plane surface charge \(\sigma(s)=-qh/[2\pi(s^2+h^2)^{3/2}]\) gives the same result. It is precisely \(-qP_h\). Thus the boundary kernel has both a voltage-producing interpretation and a charge-collection interpretation measurable with different instrumentation.

Recover the kernel of an electrode strip

A boundary pattern invariant in \(y\) should not require a two-dimensional source integral. Integrate \(P_h\) over the unused coordinate. With lateral separation \(s=X-X'\), the result is

\[p_h(s)=h/[\pi(s^2+h^2)].\]

It integrates to one over the real line. A strip between \(-b,b\) at \(V_d\) gives on its central normal \(V(0,h)=2V_d\arctan(b/h)/\pi\). At \(h=b\) the value is half the applied voltage, distinct from the circular electrode's \(1-1/\sqrt2\). The strip extends in one more direction and occupies a larger angular range of boundary influence.

For sinusoidal boundary data \(f(X)=\cos kX\) there is an independent way to determine the result without evaluating a difficult oscillatory integral. Translational symmetry and linearity preserve the sinusoid. Solving Laplace's equation with vertical factor \(Z(h)\) gives

\[Z''=k^2Z.\]

The bounded decaying solution with unit boundary amplitude is \(e^{-|k|h}\). Thus the Fourier transform of the strip kernel is \(e^{-|k|h}\). Convolving twice with heights \(h_1,h_2\) is equivalent to convolution once with height \(h_1+h_2\). This composition property checks a numerical propagation code without requiring separate analytic solutions for many different boundary patterns.

Which symmetries does an influence table possess?

Exchange source and detector in the defining equations for \(G(\mathbf x,\cdot)\) and \(G(\mathbf z,\cdot)\). Their difference identity gives \(G(\mathbf x,\mathbf z)-G(\mathbf z,\mathbf x)=0\), since both vanish on the same boundary. Symmetry extends to scalar or symmetric-tensor positive permittivity when the operator is defined as \(-\nabla\cdot\epsilon\nabla\). It refers to exactly the same material, boundary preparation and domain in both experiments. Replacing one experiment's grounded conductor by a floating conductor changes the Green function.

On a nonuniform mesh it matters whether influence entries correspond to equal point charges or equal charge densities in unequal cells. If a finite-volume solver stores sources as densities, a column's total injected charge scales with its cell volume. An unweighted table of sampled voltages can look nonsymmetric even when the discrete physics is reciprocal. Form the matrix with unit total source and consistent detector averaging, or retain the correct volume-weighted inner product. Test positivity for positive sources as a second check. Symmetric influence entries by themselves cannot expose a common incorrect normalisation or a misplaced outer boundary.

A kernel that reproduces every harmonic around a circle

In a two-dimensional circular domain of radius \(a\), prescribe a voltage \(f(\phi')\) around the wall. For a detector at \((r,\phi)\) let \(t=r/a<1\). The angular Fourier component with order \(m\) is multiplied by \(t^{|m|}\) on continuation into the disk. This follows directly from the regular radial power solution of Laplace's equation. Substitute the Fourier coefficient integral into the series and interchange the integral and the absolutely convergent series at fixed \(t<1\). The boundary response per unit angle is then

\[P_t(\delta)=\frac1{2\pi}\sum_{m=-\infty}^{\infty}t^{|m|}e^{im\delta}=\frac{1-t^2}{2\pi(1-2t\cos\delta+t^2)},\]
\[\delta=\phi-\phi' .\]

To obtain the rational form, separate the \(m=0\) term and sum the two conjugate geometric series \(te^{\pm i\delta}/(1-te^{\pm i\delta})\). The result is positive for every interior point. Integrating it over angle leaves only the constant Fourier mode, so it is normalised to unity. At the centre the kernel is simply \(1/(2\pi)\), recovering the exact mean-value theorem for harmonic functions. Near the wall it becomes very sharply concentrated around the detector's azimuth while retaining a small nonlocal tail. Using prescribed boundary data as a flat average at every interior point would reproduce only the centre reading.

For a concrete experiment put a short arc of half-angle \(\alpha\) centred on \(\phi=0\) at 1 V and ground the remaining circular boundary. Along \(\phi=0\) the potential equals the integral of \(P_t\) between \(-\alpha,\alpha\). With \(u=\tan(\phi'/2)\) one has \(\cos\phi'=(1-u^2)/(1+u^2)\), \(d\phi'=2du/(1+u^2)\). Substitution gives the weight

\[w(t)=\frac{2}{\pi}\arctan\!\left[\frac{1+t}{1-t}\tan(\alpha/2)\right] .\]

At \(t=0\) it gives \(\alpha/\pi\), exactly the fraction of the circumference occupied by the arc. For a 60°-wide arc \(\alpha=\pi/6\), hence the centre reads \(1/6\) V. At \(t=0.6\) on the centreline it reads about 0.522 V. Placing a small positive line charge there while grounding the whole cylindrical wall induces \(-0.522\) times the line charge on that arc by the same reciprocity argument used on the flat disk. The angular share depends on position inside the circle even though every point on the conductor has the same actual grounded voltage.

An independent check takes the limit of a very large circle with the probe only a distance \(h=a-r\) from the nearest boundary point. For small angular displacement \(\delta=s/a\), \(1-2t\cos\delta+t^2\simeq(h/a)^2+(s/a)^2\) and \(1-t^2\simeq2h/a\). The angular probability element \(P_t(\delta)d\delta\) therefore tends to \(h\,ds/[\pi(h^2+s^2)]\), precisely the translation-invariant strip kernel obtained by integrating the half-space formula over one coordinate. Curvature corrections can be estimated by increasing \(a/h\) at fixed physical arc width and comparing both predictions.

Why an insulated Green function needs a zero-mode subtraction

Consider the simpler interval \(0<x<L\) with prescribed zero flux at both ends. We would like a point response satisfying \(-G''=\delta(x-s)\), \(G'(0)=G'(L)=0\). Integrate this proposed equation over the interval: the left-hand side is \(-G'(L)+G'(0)=0\), whereas the right-hand side is 1. The attempted kernel violates compatibility. Physically a sealed insulating region cannot take a net free test charge without changing its prescribed outer flux. Adding an arbitrary constant to \(G\) cannot fix this contradiction.

A useful alternative removes the constant source mode and fixes the remaining voltage reference. Define \(-G_N''=\delta(x-s)-1/L\), \(G_N'(0)=G_N'(L)=0\), and \(\int_0^L G_N(x,s)\,dx=0\). Away from the point the curvature is \(+1/L\). The left derivative must be \(x/L\) to vanish at zero, the right derivative \(x/L-1\) to vanish at \(L\). They jump by \(-1\) at \(x=s\). Integrating, imposing continuity and subtracting the mean gives the compact symmetric expression

\[G_N(x,s)=\frac{x^2+s^2}{2L}-\max(x,s)+\frac L3 .\]

Differentiating verifies both end conditions and the jump. Integrating the quadratic part and the piecewise maximum over \(x\) verifies the mean condition. Its sign can be negative because the response includes an opposite uniform return source and uses a zero-mean reference. Unlike the positive grounded Dirichlet kernel, it is not a positive voltage weight to use for a maximum-principle test.

Apply a neutral pair of sheets \(+\sigma\) at \(s_1=0.2L\) and \(-\sigma\) at \(s_2=0.7L\). Their voltage is \(\sigma[G_N(x,s_1)-G_N(x,s_2)]/\epsilon\). The two artificial uniform return sources cancel exactly. To the left of both sheets the field vanishes, between them it equals \(+\sigma/\epsilon\), and to the right it vanishes again. The potential drops by \(0.5L\sigma/\epsilon\) across the two sheets. This matches direct Gauss-law jumps, and the zero-mean potential fixes the constant that Gauss's law could not determine. For more complex closed domains numerical solvers implement the same idea by projecting out the constant eigenvector and checking the compatible total charge before inverting. An arbitrary tiny diagonal regularisation of a singular matrix can hide a physically impossible net-source input.

Construct a point response when the medium itself screens charge

A linear ionic or phenomenological screening model between two grounded parallel electrodes leads in one dimension to \([-\partial_x^2+\kappa^2]V=\rho_{\rm imposed}/\epsilon\), \(0<x<L\). Here \(\rho_{\rm imposed}\) excludes the mobile linear response already represented by \(\kappa^2V\). We seek \(G_\kappa(x,s)\) with zero voltage at both ends. Away from its unit source the equation has hyperbolic solutions. Use \(A\sinh(\kappa x)\) on the left to vanish at zero and \(B\sinh[\kappa(L-x)]\) on the right to vanish at \(L\). Continuity at \(x=s\) removes one constant. Integrating across the source gives \(G'_\kappa(s^+)-G'_\kappa(s^-)=-1\); the finite mass term integrates to zero in a shrinking source interval.

To solve transparently use the functions \(u(x)=\sinh(\kappa x)\),

\[v(x)=\sinh[\kappa(L-x)].\]

Their Wronskian \(u'v-uv'\) is \(\kappa\sinh(\kappa L)\), independent of \(x\). Then continuity, the derivative jump, and symmetry under \(s\leftrightarrow x\) are manifest in

\[G_\kappa(x,s)=\frac{\sinh(\kappa x_<)\sinh[\kappa(L-x_>)]}{\kappa\sinh(\kappa L)},\]
\[x_<=\min(x,s),\]
\[x_>=\max(x,s).\]

For \(\kappa\to0\), expand each hyperbolic sine to first order. The result is \(x_<(L-x_>)/L\), the grounded unscreened response. When both observation and source lie many screening lengths from every boundary, the kernel tends to \(e^{-\kappa|x-s|}/(2\kappa)\), the infinite-line screened solution. Thus one formula contains both geometric screening by grounded conductors and material screening by mobile charges.

At the midpoint source and detector coincide. The finite kernel reduces to \(\tanh(\kappa L/2)/(2\kappa)\). Compared with the unscreened midpoint value \(L/4\), its ratio is \(2\tanh(\kappa L/2)/(\kappa L)\). At \(\kappa L=2\) a fixed inserted sheet therefore creates only 76.2% of its unscreened midpoint voltage. A naïve replacement of plate separation by the screening length would give a constant ratio and miss the smooth small-\(\kappa\) crossover. For long gaps the measured voltage saturates at fixed sheet charge, because the countercharge response near the sheet now sets the local scale.

Check the convolution using a uniform positive imposed charge density \(\rho_0\). Without integrating the kernel directly, seek a constant particular solution plus a symmetric homogeneous correction. The result is

\[V(x)=[\rho_0/(\epsilon\kappa^2)]\{1-\cosh[\kappa(x-L/2)]/\cosh(\kappa L/2)\}.\]

Twice differentiation reconstructs the source and zero endpoints. In the small-\(\kappa L\) limit it tends to \(\rho_0x(L-x)/(2\epsilon)\). A numerical integration of the point response against a uniform source should match this independent closed solution throughout the interval, including the near-electrode regions. It is a stronger test than checking the point kernel at the centre alone.

Integrate the original equation across the gap. The sum of outward displacement fluxes equals imposed charge minus \(\epsilon\kappa^2\int V\,dx\), the magnitude of the compensating mobile charge. For the uniform source the fraction of imposed charge balanced by electrode rather than mobile charge is \(2\tanh(\kappa L/2)/(\kappa L)\). For a long gap this fraction falls like twice the screening length divided by gap width, because only the two boundary regions communicate appreciably with the electrodes. A current meter attached to the electrodes and a chemical measurement of accumulated mobile ions can therefore close the same source budget independently.

Inspect the response to a localised source

Move the source while holding the boundaries fixed. Test the symmetry between source and observation point predicted by reciprocity.

Open this experiment online

Design a differential collecting electrode from its weighting functions

Two concentric electrodes supply one signed position signal

A grounded plane is divided into a central disk of radius \(a\), a surrounding annulus from \(a\) to \(2a\), and the remainder. A small charge moves along the central normal. Two charge amplifiers measure the disk and annulus. Construct an output that vanishes at \(h=a\), then find its local height sensitivity. Why can that output alone not distinguish uncertain charge from height away from the null?

Derive and check the result

Let \(w_a=1-h/\sqrt{h^2+a^2}\) and \(w_{2a}=1-h/\sqrt{h^2+4a^2}\). The disk reads \(Q_d=-qw_a\); the annulus reads \(Q_r=-q(w_{2a}-w_a)\). An output \(S=Q_d-\gamma Q_r\) vanishes at \(h=a\) if \(\gamma=(1-1/\sqrt2)/(1/\sqrt2-1/\sqrt5)=1.127\) approximately. Both readings are negative for positive \(q\), so it is their difference, not their sum, that rejects the reference position. The ideal zero does not depend on the magnitude of any nonzero \(q\).

Differentiate with respect to height using \(w_b'=-b^2/(h^2+b^2)^{3/2}\). At the null \(a\,w_a'=-1/(2\sqrt2)\) and \(a\,w_{2a}'=-4/(5\sqrt5)\). Hence

\[a\,d(S/q)/dh=-(1+\gamma)a w_a'+\gamma a w_{2a}'\simeq0.349.\]

For \(q=10\) pC, \(a=2.0\) mm, a small upward displacement of 0.10 mm produces approximately \(+0.175\) pC. Finite differences of the full two-disk formula at heights on both sides of \(a\) check the linear slope. For displacements that are no longer small compared with \(a\), use the full expression rather than extrapolating that single sensitivity.

An additional sum channel \(Q_d+Q_r=-q w_{2a}\) permits a dimensionless output \(S/(Q_d+Q_r)\) that cancels the magnitude and sign of \(q\) while retaining height dependence. This ratio becomes noise sensitive far from the electrodes, where collected charge is small. A null by itself can also mean that there is no charge at all, so a usable position detector checks the sum channel at the same time. The exercise combines electrostatic reciprocity, electronic weighting and a finite signal-to-noise constraint while requiring only two precomputed boundary responses.

Tutorial · predict before numerically integrating the kernel

How much charge belongs to an annular collector?

An annular electrode with inner radius \(h\) and outer radius \(3h\) lies in a grounded plane. A charge \(q\) sits at height \(h\) over its centre. Derive its induced charge. If a numerical integration omits all radii beyond \(3h\), where does the missing charge go?

Work through the solution

Subtract two circular weights. The annulus receives \(Q/q=-[1/\sqrt2-1/\sqrt{10}]=-0.3909\). The central disk receives \(-(1-1/\sqrt2)q=-0.2929q\). The whole plane should sum to \(-q\), so the part beyond the outer edge supplies

\[-q/\sqrt{10}=-0.3162q.\]

Nothing has been lost by the physical system; a finite integration patch excludes a considerable piece of the long-tailed kernel. Radial extrapolation with the analytic far tail or an expanding integration domain distinguishes missing area from poor quadrature within the patch.

Use resistance to a modelling mistake

A student proposes \(G\) itself as the weight for setting electrode voltages on the plane. Another replaces it by \(2P_h\), arguing that an image doubles the response. Predict two different tests that diagnose these errors.

Work through the solution

The first proposal gives zero contribution since the Dirichlet Green function is zero at the boundary. It confuses volume source response with a boundary normal derivative. The second produces twice the prescribed constant voltage because the kernel would integrate to two. Independent electrostatic reciprocity would assign \(-2q\) to the whole grounded plane around a charge \(q\), violating the charge sum derived from the real and image fields. For a numerical test, set successively a constant, a sinusoid, and a bounded disk on the same plane. Those cases examine normalisation, spectral attenuation, and spatial tails separately.
